# Changelog

All notable changes to bide are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and this project
adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html). While bide is pre-1.0, a
minor version (0.x.0) may include breaking API changes, which are marked **Breaking:** below; the
journal format can change between pre-releases without a version bump. Curated highlights for each release are in [docs/releases](docs/releases).

## [Unreleased]

Unreleased changes are in `changes/unreleased/`.

## [0.25.0] - 2026-10-26

### Added

- `bide edit -run <id>` with a store (`-db`, `-postgres`, `-postgres-env` or `-journal`, the flags `bide view` takes, opened read-only) compares the file you are editing with the topology that run journaled, in a new panel, **Compared with a run**. It says SAME DIGEST (typed mode), NO DIFFERENCE SEEN (structural mode, where kinds, types and gates are not compared), DIFFERS FROM THE RUN with the changes the topology digest covers (including a change of order), or CANNOT COMPARE, and that a run paused under a different topology will not resume under the file. It compares topologies and nothing more: it does not say the run followed the file, and safety and assert lines, which are not in a journal or the digest, are not compared. It never blocks a save and does not change the weakened banner. A run that journaled no topology, has no digest yet, or journaled a topology that does not match its digest is named as such. The run is read once and again on **Reload run**. The page also draws the run's path on the flow (a ring and words per node: ran, started with no result recorded, paused at its approval gate; a dashed line over each connection taken), laid on the file by name with the session's renames, and lists what the file cannot show; the path is read by a few point reads per node. With a store, with or without `-run`, a second panel, **Unfinished runs this file would refuse**, lists the store's unfinished runs by the digest each recorded (one small record per run, never a history; at most 5000 runs, the first by run ID, said as "at least") and says which a drive under the file (resume, recovery or restart) would refuse: a different digest, or a topology that differs by what the digest covers; runs of another flow and runs that recorded no digest yet are counted apart. `-run` is no longer required with a store. See [Compare with a run](docs/guides/flow-editor.md#compare-with-a-run).
- `plan.InspectTopology(t Topology) ConfigReport` is the report of a declared `Topology` alone: its digest, its approval gates and which nodes every path to passes through a gate, computed by the code `InspectConfig` uses. It is for a tool that holds a topology and no config, such as the topology a run journaled (`JournaledTopology`). `Safety` and `Assertions` are nil, since a topology carries neither; it does not validate (a dangling edge or a duplicate name is tolerated), never panics, costs time linear in the nodes and edges, and shares no memory with its argument. The editor's "compare with a run" (above; docs/design/visual-editor.md, "Phase 4 design") uses it.
- `agent.RaisesSafety(reason)` (experimental, with `ToolSpec.RaisesSafety`): `agent.New` now refuses with `ErrConfig` a wrapper whose declared `Safety` is safer than a tool it embeds or unwraps, at any depth (the order is a side effect, then `Idempotent`, then `ReadOnly`), naming the wrapper, the tool and both levels; a wrapper that really does make the call safer declares `RaisesSafety(reason)` (the reason follows the rules of `agent.Trusted`). The reason is in the declarations document (`raises_safety`, and `idempotent` for a retry-safe side effect) and, only when set, in the declarations digest, so existing digests are unchanged. `bide doctor -declarations` lists it and `bide doctor -diff` reports adding it as WEAKENED and removing it as strengthened. `bide doctor -diff` also reports any tool that becomes safer to re-run (side effect to `Idempotent`, or either to `ReadOnly`) as WEAKENED. Behavior change: an agent with such a wrapper (for example a `ReadOnly` wrapper over a side-effecting tool) now fails at `New` until the wrapper declares the inner tool's `Safety` or `RaisesSafety`. See docs/reference/extension-points.md and docs/guides/untrusted-content.md. The declarations format stays `bide.declarations.v1` (experimental), and every document the agent now dumps carries a `tracks_idempotent` member (and, where they apply, `idempotent` and `raises_safety`), so an older `bide doctor` (v0.24.0 and earlier) refuses a new document as having an unknown member. In the other direction, `bide doctor -diff` of a baseline dumped by an older version against a new dump that has an `Idempotent` side-effecting tool reports one WEAKENED line, asking you to regenerate the baseline with the new version, instead of reading the missing member as "not Idempotent": a pipeline regenerates its baseline once after upgrading. Behavior change, broader than `Safety`: the wrapper check on an `Unwrap` chain now also looks inside the tools each chain tool embeds, for every property it checks (approval gate, `Timeout`, `Caps`, `Untrusted` and `Safety`), so an agent whose wrapper reaches a tool by `Unwrap` that in turn embeds a gated, capped, untrusted or less safe tool, and that was accepted before, can now fail at `New` with `ErrConfig` until the wrapper declares the same.

### Changed

- Amount caps are experimental: `agent.WithCaps`, `agent.WithCapNamespace` and `ToolSpec.Caps` are marked experimental in their documentation and in the API reference, as the taint names are, and `docs/trust/stability.md` now lists every experimental name and command of v0.23.0 and v0.24.0 (the caps names, `WithTaintPolicy` and `TaintPolicy`, `GateReason`, `(*Agent).Declarations`, the `Why` fields, `bide audit caps`, `bide doctor -declarations` and `-diff`, `bide init -agent` and `-single-approver`). Under that page, experimental means the Go names and the command flags may change in a minor release with a changelog entry; a configured cap still refuses the call it covers. No behavior changes.

### Fixed

- The core module and its test code build on Solaris and illumos again (`go vet ./...` was failing in the core, `flowedit` and `cmd/bide-anchor`): the tests that make a named pipe are built only where `syscall.Mkfifo` exists (Linux, macOS, FreeBSD, NetBSD, OpenBSD, DragonFly), and `notify/relay`'s state-file lock, which used `syscall.Flock`, now builds everywhere. On Solaris and AIX (not illumos, which has `Flock`) a relay refuses a `StatePath` at start with `exclusive file locks are not supported on this platform`, as on any platform without a file lock; it never runs two relays on one file unlocked. The SQLite-backed modules (`store/sqlite`, `govern/sqlitelog`, `cmd/bide` and the modules that import them) still do not build on DragonFly, Solaris and illumos, because `modernc.org/sqlite` does not support them.
- `bide audit verify-evidence` and `bide audit verify-decision-coverage` name a step whose key holds a call ID by that ID. The line for the gate of call `c2#taint` read `step "approval-pending:c2%23taint"`, the escaped form the engine stores in the key; it now reads `step "approval-pending" of call "c2#taint"`, and a key suffix stays (`step "approval-tally:v3" of call "c2#taint"`). This covers the approval gate's keys (pending, request, tally, deadline, decision, exec hold, identity, pause, reason). A key that is not that shape, or whose ID is a digest (an ID over 96 escaped bytes), is shown as stored, and so is a notification key (`approval:<run>:<call>:<n>`), which names a run before the call. Other commands that quote a stored record name in an error, and the key of an absence proof, still show it as stored. Display only: the stored key, the package and its seal are unchanged.
- `bide view` says why a gate's signatures were not checked when the `-sth` or `-evidence` given is for another run: `signatures not checked: the proof given is for another run (signed head: "run-b"); give -sth or -evidence for this run`. It used to say `pass -approver-keys, with -sth or -evidence`, which a user who had passed all three could not act on. The note appears only when no prover made a proof for the run; a proof for this run that fails is shown as the failure it is. The run IDs are shown escaped and cut at 64 characters. Nothing about what is checked changes: a run without a proof is still unchecked.

## [0.24.0] - 2026-10-25

### Added

- The approve page lists why a call is held (the untrusted content a tainted turn read, or the cap rule that holds it, with this call's figures as of the ask) above the arguments, as text, labelled as the application's record and not part of what the approver signs, and marks the arguments as model-written and unverified (everything in them is chosen by the model). The intake's `GET /pending` answer carries the reasons as `why` (`approvals.Pending.Why`, experimental). The page bundle changes: its `approve-page.SHA256SUMS` are new, so an organisation that pins the bundle reviews the update. The signed decision (`bide.approval.v3`) is unchanged.
- Every attempt marker the engine, `ClaimAttempt` or `ResolveHalt` (for the next attempt) claims now carries `recorded_at`, the instant of the claim on the clock the run was driven with (`WithClock`, else the wall clock), readable as `Record.RecordedAt()`. The marker's `AttemptedAt` stays the wall clock, which the age of a halt is measured on, so a run clock that lags or leads does not move it. A caller cannot set it. `bide audit caps` dates a call's cap window by it. Markers written before keep their form.
- `bide audit caps` (experimental) audits the amount-cap ledgers of a store, read-only. Each ledger run must read under the ledger's own rules, and its window total must be the sum of its live reserves. Its slots must agree with the `cap-reserve` records of the runs that made the calls (a deleted slot, a changed slot, a slot no run recorded) and with each attempt's `cap-intent`, which must fit the call's own time (a planted intent that moves a slot to another window fails) and the rule's index run. A release of a call whose tool produced a result fails, and any other release must be explained by the loop's `cap-release:` record or by the attempt's void (without either it fails when anchored and warns when not). With `-sth` and `-pubkey` the ledgers and the runs' cap records must be what the newest signed heads commit to, and entries after them fail as not attested; with `-unanchored` the report says first that whoever can write the store can delete a slot or append a release undetected. It lists the slots counted over the limit under a waiver and the reconcile exposure, and `-namespace`, `-rule`, `-window` and `-run` narrow the audit and list what they left unchecked. `bide sample` now writes a small cap ledger (`bide audit caps -db sample.db -unanchored`). The amount caps guide gains a worked configuration and the cost of a ledger on an `AuditedStore`. See docs/guides/amount-caps.md.
- When the loop releases a call's cumulative-cap reservation because the tool never began, it first journals why, as a `cap-release:<attempt>` record in the run that made the call (`bide.cap-release.v1`: the attempt and the reason, a cap refusal or a chain that returned without calling the tool). A write that fails releases nothing: the slot stays counted and the reconcile pass releases it after the void, as when a release fails. The name is reserved, so `Step` and `ClaimAttempt` refuse it. `bide audit caps` holds each release to this record or to a void. A release the reconcile pass makes is justified by the void and writes no record.
- `bide doctor -declarations <file>` reports on the taint and cap declarations a host dumps from its agent (side-effecting tools with no taint policy, exempt tools, cap-carrying tools, a weak escalation gate), and `bide doctor -diff <old> <new>` lists every change between two dumps as weakened, changed or strengthened (a raised cap limit, a removed or renamed cap rule, a removed `Untrusted`, a new `TaintExempt`, a lower `need`, a requester no longer excluded) and exits 1 if anything is weakened. A document with a member this version does not know, or whose digest is not the digest of what it declares, is refused. `(*Agent).Declarations` writes the document. The first-run checks are unchanged. See docs/guides/untrusted-content.md.
- The reasons a call is gated are shown to the approver. `bide approve -list` and the decision screen print `why:` lines for the escalation gate of a tainted call (the untrusted content its turn read, where, and at which journal position), `bide view` shows them in the gate's card, `-list -json` carries `why`, and the notifier payload carries `approval.why` (a cap reason's figures only for a tool named in `Args(...)`). The reasons come from the `gate-reason` record the engine writes; they are the application's record, no gate reads them, and the signed decision does not bind them. `agent.GateReason` (the type of one reason, experimental) and the `Why` fields of `agent.NotifyApproval` and `notify.Approval` are new.
- `bide init -agent` scaffolds an agent project instead of a plan flow: a tool that returns an email and declares it untrusted (`agent.Untrusted`), a refund, and an `agent.WithTaintPolicy` whose escalation gate is two of three signed decisions that do not count the requester (`ExcludePrincipal`), so a side effect the model asks for after reading the email waits for a human decision. `-single-approver` makes the gate one unsigned decision for a team of one (weaker, and the project's README says so). The project runs with no API key, checks its own story in `go test`, and writes `declarations.json` for `bide doctor -declarations`. The plan-flow scaffold is unchanged; the taint policy does not cover plan flows. New flags: experimental. `bide init` also refuses (a usage error, nothing written) a module name with an empty element, an element that begins or ends with a dot or holds `..`, or text spelled like a marker of its project template; its templates are filled in one pass.
- `agent.TaintPolicy.RequireProvenance` (experimental) makes the taint policy refuse a tool that states no provenance: a tool must declare `Untrusted` or the new `agent.Trusted(reason)` (the tool's results are the host's own; the reason is required, goes in the declarations digest, and writes no mark; `ToolSpec.Trusted` is the same declaration), else `agent.New` returns `ErrConfig` naming the tool. A new `Run`, `Send` or `SendOnce` must state `WithUntrustedInput` or `WithTrustedInput`, else `ErrConfig` at its first drive with nothing written (a sub-agent's run and a resume are not asked). The declarations document gains `require_provenance` and `trusted`; `bide doctor -diff` reports turning the requirement off, or replacing `Untrusted` by `Trusted`, as WEAKENED, and `-declarations` lists a tool with no provenance when the policy does not require one. The digest of a policy that does not set it is unchanged. The `bide init -agent` scaffold sets it. `Trusted`, `ToolSpec.Trusted` and `RequireProvenance` are experimental. See docs/guides/untrusted-content.md.
- Taint now crosses run boundaries when a taint policy is in force (experimental). A sub-agent started by a call of a tainted turn records the marks that turn was shown in its `run:start` (`ext.taint_inherited`) and starts tainted; a sub-agent whose run ended with a mark marks the parent's `tool_result` for the call (`subagent:<tool name>`), so the parent's next turn is tainted; and a session turn that ended with marks records them in the session journal (`taint/turn/<n>`), so every later turn starts with them. Runs that are not under taint record and pass nothing, and their journals are as before. No exported name is added. See docs/guides/untrusted-content.md.
- `agent.WithTaintPolicy` and `agent.TaintPolicy` (experimental): a run that read content marked untrusted needs an escalation gate for each later side-effecting call (or, with `Refuse`, is refused), in addition to the tool's own gate. The gate is a second instance on the call, under the subject `<call>#taint`, decided by its own approvers; an approval clears nothing. The approvals intake, `bide approve`, `bide approve -list`, `bide view` and the audit evidence decide and show the instance by the gate its pause note records, never by the tool's own gate. Sub-agents and sessions inherit taint, in this release, as the taint inheritance entry describes. See the guide in docs/guides/untrusted-content.md.

### Fixed

- A model stream that is cut partway through an event (a dropped connection) now ends with the read error, retried as before, in every adapter that reads SSE (Anthropic, OpenAI, Gemini). Before, the half event the cut left was decoded first, and the turn failed with a misleading "unexpected end of JSON input" decode error in place of the failed read. A reply over the response cap was already handled this way.

## [0.23.2] - 2026-10-25

### Fixed

- `ClaimAttempt` stamps a marker's `AttemptedAt` from the wall clock again. v0.23.1 stamped it from the clock bound to the context (`WithClock`), so under a run clock that lagged the wall clock a marker claimed this instant looked old: `ResolveHalt` with `WithMinHaltAge` resolved it at once, and the notifier's sweep reported it as a halt before `WithNotifyHaltAge`, defeating the grace period they enforce. The engine's own claims for a tool call and a `Journal.Step` were always stamped from the wall clock. The age checks use the wall clock unless the resolver is given its own clock, so they see the true age again. Without `WithClock` nothing changes.

## [0.23.1] - 2026-10-24

### Fixed

- The cap reconcile pass (`Recover`, `RecoverLoop`) read the wall clock to decide whether a day or hour window was still open, while a run reserves by its own clock. It now reads the clock bound to its context, else the wall clock. No application could bind one to `Recover`, so a deployment saw no change in behavior; the wall-clock judgement and its over-count direction for `WithClock` agents are documented in `Recover`, `RecoverLoop` and the known limitations. (#512)

### Security

- `agent.ClaimAttempt` now claims bare attempt markers only; it returns `ErrConfig` and writes nothing for anything else. Before, v0.23.0 and earlier let a caller with journal access plant records through it: a tool result (the next drive completed the call with the forged result and never ran the tool), a single-approver approval (the next drive ran the gated tool), and a record under a name the engine reserves, such as a `cap-intent:` that pinned a cap reservation to an old window and so bypassed the cap, a `gate-reason:` or an `approval-pending:`. Now a name under a reserved prefix (`cap-intent:`, `cap-reserve:`, `gate-reason:`, `approval-pending:`, `slot:`, `taint/` and the rest that `Step` refuses, except `attempt:`), the engine-derived `attempt:not-started:` and `attempt:retry:` forms, an empty name, a record whose kind is not `attempt`, a caller-set `AttemptedAt` (the claim stamps it from the context's clock now (changed in v0.23.2: the wall clock again); before, a caller could back-date a marker so a halt looked old enough for `ResolveHalt`'s `WithMinHaltAge`), and an attempt record that carries a result, an approval, a model turn or a reconciliation are all refused. The engine's own paths (the halt resolution's claim included) do not go through the exported call and are unchanged. An approval of an m-of-n gate was never affected, since only signed decisions count. A caller with journal access can still pre-claim an `attempt:tool:<call>` marker, which halts the call and cannot admit it or release a cap.

## [0.23.0] - 2026-10-23

### Added

- CI: a "Test (FIPS 140-3)" job runs the Go tests of the modules with `GODEBUG=fips140=only` against Go's in-tree FIPS module and module v1.26.0 (in the merge queue, on main and on a pull request labelled `full-ci`) and against module v1.0.0 (on main), with Postgres and Redis service containers and a live Redis and Postgres test required to pass. Not run under the mode: the SoftHSM2 and PKCS#11 suites, the MinIO Object Lock suite (`anchor/awss3`), the cloud KMS and GCS emulator suites, the `step-ca` container recipe, the browser-driven suites (they have their own Chrome jobs) and the exhaustive or corpus-rewriting tests. Under v1.0.0, which has no ML-DSA, it skips the tests listed in `.github/fips-v1.0.0-excluded.txt` and nothing else, and a check fails when a listed test no longer fails with that error. It is not a required check. New tests in `internal/fipsmode` and `cmd/bide/internal/approvecmd` pin that the approved schemes (Ed25519, ECDSA P-256) work under the mode and that ML-DSA or hybrid under v1.0.0 is an error, not a panic. The documentation states what is tested; bide still makes no claim of FIPS validation.
- Cumulative amount caps: `agent.WithCaps` now accepts `sum N CUR per run|hour|day [by key] -> refuse` (a tool that carries one reserves each call's amount in a cap ledger, a reserved journal run under `@cap/`, after its attempt is claimed and before the tool begins, and the call that would take the window over its limit is refused as a known failure the model reads, with the tool not called), and `agent.WithCapNamespace` names the deployment's ledgers (default `default`). A store that cannot be read or written fails the call before the tool is begun, so a cap is fail closed; an amount of zero reserves nothing; a negative, absent or unparseable amount, a missing key, or a key with a byte outside ASCII is refused. A call that may have run stays counted, so a window only over-counts. The loop releases the slot of a not-retry-safe call it proved never began (it seals the call's began word first, and releases before it records the attempt as not started), and `Recover` and `RecoverLoop` release the slot of an attempt recorded as not started; a call that began, an `Idempotent` call, a `ResolveHalt`ed call and a saga's rollback are never released. `-> gate` is still refused at registration. See the amount caps guide.
- Per-call amount caps: `agent.WithCaps(lines...)` declares rules on a tool (`name: tools.argument[minor, currency=member] > 500 USD -> refuse`), and `ToolSpec` carries `Caps` for a tool that builds its own spec. The rules are checked against the tool's input schema when the tool is registered (an argument the schema lacks, one that is an array, object or boolean, a currency member that is not a string, a limit above 2^53 minor units, a rule with `-> gate` or `-> gate(name)`, which arrives with the escalation gate, and a cumulative `sum` rule, which needs the cap ledger, are `ErrConfig` and "not yet supported"), and every call is decided twice: on the arguments the model sent, before any gate or claim, where a refused call is answered with a tool error that names the rule and gives no figure, and again on the arguments the tool will receive after any tool middleware, where a call a middleware pushed over the limit is refused before the call begins. An amount that cannot be read with certainty (negative, absent, an exponent form, too many fraction digits, or arguments with a repeated or case-variant member) gets the rule's action, and a currency no rule covers gets the strictest action among the argument's rules, compared byte for byte; no float is used. The reasons are recorded in `gate-reason:<call>`, which can add a requirement and never remove one. A tool that carries caps cannot be a plan flow block: `plan.Registry.RegisterTool` and `Builder.Tool` return `ErrConfig`. See [amount caps](docs/guides/amount-caps.md) and the limits in `KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` (array shapes, no conversion, no totals). Break risk: none for existing code; the new `ToolSpec` field is nil for a tool that does not use it.
- Reserved names for the cap and taint work that follows, and `agent.ErrToolUseIDInvalid`. Run IDs that start with `@cap/` are reserved for cap ledger runs: `Run`, `Stream`, `RunTyped` and `Session` return `ErrConfig` for one (`Run`, `Stream` and `RunTyped` already did for `@stop`; `Session` still accepts that id), as do the exported calls that write records to a run you name (`Approve`, `SubmitDecision`, `Cancel`, `ResolveHalt`, `AnswerInterrupt`, `Signal`, `Enqueue`, `Ack`, `ClaimAttempt`, `Step`, `Parallel`, `Lease` and the `audit.Record*` writers; reads such as `History` and `Status` are not refused, and these calls keep their existing behaviour for `@stop`), and `agent.IsReservedRun` (the stop control run or a cap ledger) and `agent.IsCapRun` report them; `Recover`, `RecoverLoop`, the notification sweep and its backfill, the run viewer, `bide approve -list`, `bide audit notifications` and `bide audit redactions` skip such a run. Step names that start with `slot:`, `cap-intent:`, `cap-reserve:`, `gate-reason:` or `taint/` are reserved as well (`Step`, `Parallel` and `ResolveHalt` return `ErrConfig`, `IsReservedStepName` reports them, and redaction treats them as protected). A live model turn whose tool-use ID holds `#` now fails with `ErrToolUseIDInvalid`, which wraps `ErrModel` and `ErrProtocol` (so `middleware.Retry` and `middleware.Retryable` retry it, and the rejected turn is not journaled); resuming a journal that already holds such an ID does not re-check it, but `agent.Replay` used as the model of a new run treats the recorded turns as live and fails with `ErrToolUseIDInvalid` on a recorded `#` ID. Break risk: a deployment that already runs a run ID under `@cap/` can no longer start it, and the existing run is no longer recovered, listed by the viewer or `bide approve -list`, or read by the audit verbs, so a gate waiting in it would not be shown to an approver; rename such a run before upgrading. A `Step` or `Parallel` task named with one of these step prefixes in a run that resumes fails with `ErrConfig`, and a model server that sends `#` in a tool-use ID fails every turn that calls a tool.
- Untrusted-content marks (experimental, part of the design in `docs/design/untrusted-content-and-amount-caps.md`): `agent.Untrusted(source)` declares that a tool's results are untrusted content, and the engine records the source with every result (`Safety.Untrusted` of the result record, including denials, errors and a saga's failure record); `agent.WithUntrustedInput` and `agent.WithTrustedInput` state the provenance of a run's input in `run:start`'s settings, and a later drive that passes another is `ErrConfig`; `agent.RetrievalUntrusted` marks a retrieval layer's step; `agent.TaintExempt(reason)` is a Go-only exemption declaration. Marks are recorded with or without a taint policy and gate nothing yet. A tool that embeds or unwraps an `Untrusted` tool and does not carry the declaration is refused at registration (as for an approval gate, a timeout or caps), `Step` refuses a `Safety` that names an untrusted source, and a tool that declares `Untrusted` or `TaintExempt` is refused as a plan flow node until flows have a taint rule. Records without marks are byte for byte as before; older binaries ignore the new members.

### Changed

- CI: a push to main that is one commit changing only documentation or the changelog now skips the Go test jobs, as a pull request does (Lint, the docs checks and the changelog check still run, and the CI run still completes with success, so the release script's gates read it as before; the release script now checks the push run of the nearest commit at or below the release commit that changes code, so a documentation commit on a red code commit cannot be tagged). The commit is diffed against the push's before SHA; a push of several commits, a force push or a first push runs everything.
- CI: Test (windows-latest) is now skipped on every run that changes no code, not only on a pull request: a documentation-only push to main (and a documentation-only merge queue batch) no longer starts the Windows job that the Go jobs' skip was meant to save. The decision moved into `.github/scripts/windows-decide.sh`, whose self-test runs in CI and covers each event and a failed diff. A skipped job still satisfies the required check, and any doubt still runs it.
- CI: Test (windows-latest) now also runs on a pull request that changes a test file, testdata or a golden file, .gitattributes, a file that is not Go or documentation (go.mod, scripts, workflows), or a Go file named *_windows.go, with a go:build line naming windows or negating a constraint (!linux, !unix), or importing runtime, os, path, path/filepath, io/fs, syscall, mime, encoding or golang.org/x/sys, so Windows-only failures (line endings, MIME types, file identity, signals) show before the merge queue. Any other pull request still skips it, which satisfies the required check; the merge queue and main run it on every change as before.

### Fixed

- `bide edit`: an `assert` line the grammar cannot read is now judged to name a node by one rule, whatever punctuation surrounds the name. The name counts when it stands in the line as a whole word or span, with a space, any punctuation or any symbol (not a letter, digit or combining mark) on each side, so `[audit]`, `audit-`, `audit/x`, `*audit*`, `_audit_`, `audit|`, a curly apostrophe or guillemets all name `audit`, `audits` and `audit2` do not, and a node name with spaces or punctuation is found as written. Removing or renaming a node such a line names is refused and names the line to fix first. The guide now says the refusal is a convenience and not a safety boundary: an unreadable line is never an assertion the check reads and stays reported as an error.
- `bide edit`: removing a node no longer leaves `assert` lines that name it behind to report as broken. The removal dialog (the `remove_node` dry run) now lists the assertions that name the node, and the confirmed removal takes them with the edges and arms, so the confirmation covers exactly what goes. Removing an assertion is a weakening, so the weakened banner names each removed line and the save dialog asks for the weakening confirmation as for any removed assertion; Undo restores the node, its wiring and its assertions. An assert line the grammar cannot read is never guessed at: if one of its words (punctuation trimmed) is the node's name, the removal is refused and says which line to fix or remove first, the same rule a rename follows; a line with no such word is left alone.
- `bide edit`: renaming a gated node no longer reads as a weakening when it is combined with another change to that node. The editor now keeps the renames made in the editing session (old name to new name, in order; an Undo takes a rename back with its step) and compares the text with the baseline read as if those renames had been made in it, so the renamed node is matched to its baseline self, while a rename together with a lowered need, a lowered safety, a removed gate or a removed `assert` line is still reported, and a rename onto a name the baseline holds for a different node is not honoured. The page sends the list with each request and the verdict names the renames it matched (new exported API in `flowedit`: `Rename`, `CompareRenamed`, `Weakening.Renamed`, and `Edited.Renames`); the saved file and `bide check -diff` are unchanged (the classifier already paired a node renamed with the same block, kind and edges, so a pure rename was not reported by either; the guide and `KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` said it was, and now say what is true: CI cannot know a rename and still reports one made beside another change to that node).
- Three paths no longer panic under `GODEBUG=fips140=only`: importing `govern/redislog` (go-redis hashed its script with SHA-1 in package init; the script now uses `NewScriptServerSHA`, so Redis computes the digest and an append still works after `SCRIPT FLUSH`, a restart or a Redis that refuses SCRIPT), verifying an RFC 3161 token that carries a SHA-1 `signingCertificate` attribute (DigiCert's tokens do; the consistency comparison now runs with enforcement off and is still made), and a webhook secret shorter than 14 bytes (under that mode `notify.NewWebhook` returns `agent.ErrConfig` wrapping the new `notify.ErrShortSecret`, a delivery that a secret cannot sign is not sent, `notify.Verify` and `notify.VerifyBody` return it, `notify.Sign` leaves the secret out, `notify.CheckSecret` is the rule, and `relay.New` and the `bide notify` verbs refuse the secret at start; other deployments are unchanged, and 32 bytes or more is recommended). Correction: the documentation said FIPS mode and ML-DSA are mutually exclusive; that holds only for Go's FIPS 140-3 module v1.0.0 (`crypto/mldsa` is unavailable there) and not for v1.26.0 and later, where ML-DSA and hybrid signing work under `GODEBUG=fips140=only`. bide makes no claim of FIPS validation.

### Security

- A tool that wraps another through `Unwrap` and does not carry an `Untrusted` declaration of the wrapped tool (the option, or a source in `Safety`) is now refused at registration with `ErrConfig`, as one that weakens the wrapped tool's approval gate, timeout or cap rules is (#508). Before, a wrapper that held the inner tool in a named field and wrote its own `Spec` registered with the declaration gone. A wrapper that neither embeds the inner tool nor implements `Unwrap` is still not seen.
- `agent.New`, `With` and the plan registry's `RegisterTool` now refuse, with `ErrConfig`, a tool wrapper (a tool with an `Unwrap() Tool` method) whose own `Spec` weakens the approval gate, the `Timeout` or the cap rules (`Caps`) of a tool on its `Unwrap` chain, however it holds that tool (a named field was not seen before: the outer `Spec` won, so a gate the wrapped tool declared silently did not apply). Weaker means no gate, fewer decisions needed, a `SingleApproval` over an m-of-n gate, an approver the wrapped gate does not name, no `ExcludePrincipal` where the wrapped gate has it, no gate deadline or a longer one, for the tool's `Timeout` none or a longer one, and for `Caps` a rule line the wrapped tool has that the wrapper does not carry word for word. This changes behaviour for a wrapper that dropped or loosened a declaration: declare the same on the wrapper, or build its spec from the wrapped tool's. Not checked through the chain: `Safety` (a wrapper may legitimately raise it, so the rule needs a design decision; until then not declaring a weaker `Safety` than the wrapped tool is the wrapper author's duty, as a `ReadOnly` claim over a side-effecting tool loses at-most-once protection), and `Untrusted` is checked by its own entry. Also fixed: a wrapper whose `Unwrap` returns a typed nil pointer no longer panics registration or sub-agent lookup; it ends the chain.

## [0.22.0] - 2026-10-21

### Added

- bide-anchor reads the Google Cloud Storage anchor with -backend gcs, taking -project (the project number) in place of -owner: verify and export work offline on a downloaded copy, and check and export read the bucket with read-only credentials, with the same exit codes as for S3 (1 for a finding, including a generation that vanished between the listing and the read, 3 when the audit could not finish, never 0 after an aborted audit). anchor/gcs gains the ErrWrongProject sentinel for a bucket another project owns (it is still an agent.ErrConfig error), which the command reports as a finding. With -backend gcs, -kms-key and -retention state the key and retention the anchor was configured with, so a bucket whose default key was swapped or whose locked retention is shorter is a finding (the S3 backend refuses the flags, since its reader checks neither). The command module now requires anchor/gcs, and a test checks the S3 and GCS object layouts cannot take each other's objects.
- `bide check`, `bide view -config` and `bide check -diff` take `-blocks <file>` (a block it holds that the config does not use is not reported; with `-diff`, `-old-blocks` describes the earlier version, which otherwise is checked structurally) (and `-old-blocks <file>` with `-diff`): a description of the program's registered blocks, written with `json.Marshal` of `plan.Registry.Describe()`. The check then covers block, predicate and merge names, types, safety, the approval gates blocks declare in Go and the digest, and a block swapped for one that declares no gate is reported as a weakening. The result is labelled as per the description file, not checked against the program.
- bide edit can add, remove and rename a node and write the entry (the first step of the editor's structure phase). Each is a verified splice like the gate and safety forms: the result is checked against the data the operation should produce, computed from the operation alone. A rename rewrites the node's name in the entry, edges, switches, arms, `else`, join inputs and `assert` lines (the last through the assertion grammar, refusing a line it cannot rewrite), and a rename of a gated node reads as weakened because the gate comparison follows names. A removal first lists what else goes (the edges, arms and comments that name the node) and is refused unless the same list is confirmed; `assert` lines naming the node are left in place for the check to report. The editor never changes the entry node without being asked: removing the entry, or the first node of a config with no `entry` key, is refused with a pointer to set_entry. The API gains the operations `add_node`, `remove_node`, `rename_node` and `set_entry`, and `Operation.DryRun`, `Operation.Confirm` and `Applied.Cascade`.
- bide edit can add an edge by dragging from the small circle at a node's right edge in the flow onto another node (the last step of the editor's structure phase). It sends the same add_edge operation the inspector's Connections form does, so it has the same refusals, verification and weakened banner, and it is an extra: the circle is hidden from assistive technology and not in the Tab order, every connection can be made and removed from the inspector by keyboard, and dropping on the same node, on nothing or pressing Escape cancels with no change (as does losing the pointer or a redraw mid-drag). It works with a mouse, a pen or a finger (the touch target is at least 44 px, and a touch elsewhere on the flow still scrolls the page). The layout stays derived from the topology; nothing about where a node is drawn is stored.
- bide edit can set an arm's loop bound and add, change and remove joins (the fourth step of the editor's structure phase). A loop bound is a number above 0 on a switch arm (0 removes it); a join is added from a disclosure under the outline (a name, a merge block and its inputs) and edited on its own inspector: the merge block, the inputs in order (add, move, remove), the same safety and approval gate forms a node has (written on the join's wiring element), and edges out of it. Removing a join lists what goes with it (the edges out of it, a switch on it, its comments) and needs that list confirmed, as removing a node does, and a node that is an input of a join is still not removed. The editor checks what the config says (an input is a declared node or join, named once, not the join itself); it cannot check that a merge block is registered or that its arity and types fit the inputs, which plan.Load does with the registry. New joins are written as `inputs: [a, b]` where the file has none to copy. The keyboard test no longer walks the whole page to the assertion form: it uses the skip links. The API gains the operations add_join, remove_join, set_join_inputs, set_join_merge and set_loop_max, Operation.Inputs and Operation.Max, and set_safety, set_approval and clear_approval accept a join's name.
- bide edit can reorder nodes and wiring elements and set the flow's name and its in and out type names (the second step of the editor's structure phase). A move exchanges two neighbouring list items as one splice (new engine operation Swap): each item keeps its own text, the comments directly above it and its trailing comment byte for byte, and whatever lies between them stays; the result is checked independently of the splice (the data with the two items exchanged, the same lines in another order, every line outside the pair where it was, each item with the comments it had), and moving back restores the exact bytes. A move into or out of the first place is refused when the config has no entry key, since the first node is then the entry; write the entry first. The wiring list is one Tab stop with a single Move pair for the selected element, and the flow settings are a closed disclosure, so the page does not cost a Tab stop per element. The API gains the operations move_node, move_wiring, set_flow, set_in and set_out, and View.In and View.Out.
- `bide edit -blocks <file>` and `flowedit.WithRegistry` / `flowedit.WithBlocks` make the flow editor typed. The editor then checks the config as `Load` does (by type name for a description file, labelled as not checked against the program), refuses an operation that connects what does not fit, adds the approval gate and safety a block declares in Go to the weakened banner without ever saying less than the structural check (so changing a block to one with no gate reads as the gate removed, and the structural "possibly" stays), and shows the digest, a palette of the program's blocks (a search, a filter for what fits the selected node, one Tab stop) and each node's types on the page; a connection by form, by drag or from the palette that does not fit is refused with plan's words, a target whose types differ is marked, and a text with errors against the blocks shows a NOT CERTIFIED banner. A port's drag handle no longer reaches over a neighbouring node in a dense or scaled-down flow.
- bide edit can add and remove edges, make a node a switch and remove the switch, and add, change, move and remove arms and set the else (the third step of the editor's structure phase), from the inspector's Connections section. Targets are chosen from the declared nodes; the editor refuses what plan.Load refuses (an edge or arm to an undeclared node, an edge into a join, an edge from a switched node, a switch on a node with an edge out, a self edge, a duplicate edge or arm, a switch with no arm: the only arm is removed with the switch). A new edge or arm that reaches a gated node by another way shows the weakened banner, from the same classifier as bide check -diff. New wiring is written in the style of the file, and as edge: [a, b] and when: [{pred: p, to: t}] where the file has none to copy; a plain scalar with a quote character inside a flow list is now written quoted, so the engine can edit its own output. The API gains the operations add_edge, remove_edge, add_switch, remove_switch, add_arm, remove_arm, set_arm, move_arm and set_else, Operation.Pred and Operation.To, and View.Wires.
- `plan.AllowUnusedBlocks()`, an option for `plan.InspectConfig` and `plan.InspectConfigDescribed` (both now take `...InspectOption`): a registered block the config does not use is no problem. Without it such a block is an error and the report has no kinds, types, safety or digest for the flow, because the check stops before it builds anything. `Load` and `Validate` still refuse an unused block. It is for tools that list the blocks a config could use, such as the flow editor's palette. Source-compatible, except for code that stores `InspectConfig` or `InspectConfigDescribed` in a variable of func type. Experimental for `InspectConfigDescribed`.
- `plan.InspectConfigDescribed` checks a flow config against a `RegistryDescription` instead of a Registry, with the same code `InspectConfig` runs: blocks, predicates, merges, types, safety, approval gates and the digest are checked without the program that registered them. `RegistryDescription.Validate` reports why a description read from a file is not one this release reads. Types are told apart by name only, so two distinct types spelled the same are one type, and messages write a type with its package path. Experimental: may change in any 0.x release.
- `plan.ConfigReport` has a new `Findings` list: for each error in `Problems`, a `plan.Finding` with a `Kind` (`plan.ProblemEdgeTypes`, `ProblemArmTypes`, `ProblemJoinInputTypes`, `ProblemTerminalTypes`, `ProblemSafetyAboveRegistration`, the loop kinds `ProblemLoopNoExitArm`, `ProblemLoopMoreThanOneBackArm`, `ProblemLoopHeadNotAncestor`, `ProblemLoopBodyNotSimple`, `ProblemLoopInnerSwitch`, `ProblemLoopEdgeLeaves`, `ProblemLoopEdgeEnters`, or `ProblemOther`), the full names it is about, `Got` and `Want` where the kind has them, and the message. A tool tells problems apart by kind and names instead of reading messages, which shorten long names and name a wiring element by its position. Findings are complete (not capped at the first hundred type problems), are found beside any other error, and every error has exactly one; `ConfigProblem` is unchanged. A kind's name, names order and `Got` and `Want` formats are stable in 0.x, kinds may be added, and `other` may become a specific kind in any release, so treat it like an unknown kind. See the plan flows guide (Findings). `bide edit` now reads findings instead of internal records. Findings are in the Go API only: `bide check` and the JSON of the viewer and editor pages do not carry them. The errors of `Load` and `Validate` carry no kinds; call `InspectConfig` or `InspectConfigDescribed` for them. The node, edge or arm of a problem is set where the problem is made, no longer read from its message, so a node whose name holds the message's punctuation is attributed exactly.
- `Registry.Describe` returns a `RegistryDescription` of what a Registry has registered: each block's name, kind, input and output type names, declared safety and approval gate, each predicate's switched type, each merge's input types, and any registration errors. It is data only (no functions or reflect values), sorted by name, and is the first part of the visual editor's typed mode. Experimental: it may change in any 0.x release.
- The theme file format for bide's visual pages and bide theme check: a theme is a small JSON file of typed values (colours as #rgb or #rrggbb, fonts from a fixed list, numbers in ranges) that names a shipped base and overrides some of it, with strict keys (an unknown, repeated or differently cased key is an error). bide theme check parses a file and measures the resolved theme: WCAG text contrast 4.5:1 and non-text contrast 3:1 (the focus ring and the control edge on the page, a panel, a nested panel and the graph canvas, and the focus ring also on a node's fill and the selected item's tint), CIEDE2000 distance between the status colours (ok, warning, alert, info at least 20; the gated, side-effect, idempotent and idle colours at least 10 from every other), a colour-blindness check (a warning for your theme; the shipped themes must pass it), and a polarity check (alert warm and nearer red than warning, ok not). It exits 1 on a theme that fails. See docs/guides/themes.md and docs/design/themes.md.
- bide edit draws from the checked themes, as bide view does. It takes -theme file.json (a custom theme that must pass bide theme check, refused before anything is served if it does not) and -theme-default name, its Theme button steps through light, dark, high-contrast and your theme (naming the next one and announcing the change to a screen reader), a reader whose system asks for more contrast gets high-contrast and one whose system forces colors gets the system's colors, and a program that embeds the editor can lock it to one theme (flowedit.WithThemeLock), which hides the button. The page's own colors now follow the contrast rules: the primary button's text is the theme's on_accent color, the edges of buttons, inputs and the source box use border_strong (3:1 against what they sit on, where the old 1.4:1 hairline did not reach it), every text control, wherever a form puts it, takes the theme's surface and edge, the dialog dims the page with a shadow of the theme's scrim color around it (it needs no support for custom properties in ::backdrop), its box keeps the theme's elevation and a border_strong edge and scrolls inside the screen when its content is tall (forced colors drop shadows, so there it keeps its border and does not dim), and the selected node in the graph is drawn with a 7 px ring in the focus color over its state (its fill and dashes still tell the state; before, a selected gated node was not marked in the graph at all). The Editor (Chrome) job now measures these colors in every shipped theme and system mode, and runs axe-core in high-contrast as well as light and dark.
- A Go program can choose the themes of the pages it embeds. The theme package (github.com/bide-ai/bide/theme, 0.x API; it was internal) parses and checks a theme file as bide theme check does (Parse, Load, Check, Report, Shipped, Lookup), and planview.Handler, planview.DiffHandler and flowedit.New take planview.WithThemes, WithDefaultTheme and WithThemeLock (the same three in flowedit): extra themes beside light, dark and high-contrast, the theme a reader starts in, and a lock that makes one theme the only one (no switch, no remembered choice). A host passes only values that passed the checks: it cannot give CSS or a selector, the stylesheet generators are not exported, and a reader whose system asks for more contrast or forces colors still gets those over a default and over a lock (a Chrome test holds both). Upgrading: planview.Handler and planview.DiffHandler now end in a variadic opts ...Option, so a call is unchanged but code that holds either function as a value of the old func type (a struct field, a variable, an interface method) no longer compiles; wrap the call in a closure. A bad set of themes (a nil or foreign theme, a repeated name, an unknown default or lock) panics when planview builds the handler and makes flowedit.New return an error. Measure has a String method, the line bide theme check prints for a measure.

### Changed

- The awss3 Reader now audits the bucket itself: Audit reads its versioning, Object Lock configuration and default encryption and reports a finding for versioning that is not enabled, Object Lock off, a missing default retention or one under 24 hours, and, through the new ReaderConfig.KMSKeyID, Retention and Mode expectations, a default retention shorter than configured, a default mode weaker than configured, a default encryption that is not the expected KMS key, and an object that is not encrypted with it or is retained for less than the expected retention from its own write; bide-anchor -backend s3 takes the same as -kms-key, -retention and -lock-mode (a KMS alias is refused as the expected key, since S3 reports the key an alias resolves to and the check would match any key: pass the key ARN). The shipped reader policy gains exactly the three read actions this needs (s3:GetBucketVersioning, s3:GetBucketObjectLockConfiguration, s3:GetEncryptionConfiguration), so a reader whose policy lacks them now gets an access error on the bucket read, and bide-anchor check exits 3 (state unknown) instead of passing: add the actions to the reader role.
- awss3.New now accepts only a key ID (a lower-case UUID), a multi-Region key ID (mrk- and 32 lower-case hex digits) or a key ARN as Config.KMSKeyID, and refuses everything else, an alias (alias/name or an alias ARN) above all, with an error wrapping agent.ErrConfig, because S3 reports the ARN of the key an alias resolves to and the read-back that checks each head's key could not tell which key it was: an alias used to match any key. Upgrading: a deployment configured with an alias must switch Config.KMSKeyID to the key ARN, or New refuses to start with the error `awss3: Config.KMSKeyID: KMS key must be a key ID or key ARN (got "alias/..."): a lower-case UUID, mrk- and 32 lower-case hex digits, or arn:<partition>:kms:<region>:<account>:key/<one of those>, with no spaces or other characters; for an alias, pass the ARN of the key it points to: config`, which you can search your logs for; an upper-case key ID, a key ID with a leading or trailing space and any other string that is not one of those forms are refused the same way (a key ID or key ARN in the forms above works as before). The Reader (ReaderConfig.KMSKeyID) and bide-anchor -kms-key apply the same allow-check. The key is an AWS KMS key, so a deployment on an S3-compatible server that was configured with a key name of that server (MinIO with a KES key, say) must switch to Config.EncryptionNone, which means the anchor does not check which key encrypts the heads.
- The flow editor guide now shows typed mode (the Blocks region, the typed tag and the digest, the NOT CERTIFIED banner and the save dialog with its two confirmation boxes) and the themes guide shows one page in light, dark, high-contrast and a custom theme. `scripts/shots.sh` regenerates them, and the approve page images, in its default run.
- bide edit's Undo and Redo are now tested over every kind of structure edit (the last step of the editor's structure phase before drag to connect): a node added, renamed and moved, an edge, a switch, a loop bound, a join, the flow's name and a gate removed. A Chrome test walks the history back to the file's exact text one step at a time, checks that the weakened banner follows each text (the server computes it again for every step), walks it forward again, and checks that the history survives a save (Undo past a save says `unsaved changes`, and Redo back to the saved text says `saved`). The behaviour is unchanged from phase 1; the guide says what it covers.
- gcs.LoadDir now uses the directory loader shared in audit/anchorobj (Layout.LoadDir) instead of its own copy: no change in behaviour (an empty directory, equivocation in one partition, a stray file, a symbolic link and an oversized file are still errors), and, as awss3.LoadDir already does, a path that is not a directory is an error wrapping ErrObject.
- The Google Cloud Storage real-run runbook and known limitations now record the teardown of the 2026-10-17 run: a delete after the retention ends goes through soft delete (the objects stay listed for the soft delete period), a bucket whose objects are only soft-deleted can be deleted, the Cloud Storage lien on project deletion is not removed when the bucket is deleted and has to be removed by hand before the project can be deleted, and the key version is destroyed after its 30-day delay while the key and key ring remain.
- `plan.LoadReader` reads at most 16 MiB and refuses a larger config with an error wrapping `agent.ErrConfig`, without reading past the byte that shows it is too large. It used to read its reader to the end however long that was, so a reader that never ended (an HTTP body, a pipe) filled memory. No real config is near the limit; to load a larger one, read it yourself and call `plan.Load`. The viewer's config readers (`bide view -config`, `-diff`, and the checks that share them) apply the same 16 MiB limit to a flow config file or a `-diff` git revision; they allowed 256 MiB before. `bide edit` already refuses a config over 1 MiB. A `-diff` git revision is read up to the limit and `git show` is stopped there, rather than buffered whole before the size check; a named pipe given as a config file is refused instead of blocking.
- A config approval block on a node whose registered block already declares an approval gate is now reported as one problem that says how to fix it (keep one, by removing approval from the config or the gate from the block) by Load, Validate and InspectConfig, and Build words a plan.Approval on a Tool with its own gate the same way; before, InspectConfig listed two problems for the one cause, neither with a remedy. The cause was that InspectConfig split an error's text at "; ", so any problem whose text held "; " (a node named "a; b", or a message with one in it) was cut in two and attached to no node: assemble, Build and the graph checks now carry their problems as a list that InspectConfig reads part by part, and the "; " workarounds in the loop and wiring checks are gone. The text of the errors Load and Validate return is unchanged, and the problems bide check and the viewer show that held "; " (such as "is fed by [...]; combine several producers with a Join") now keep Build's own wording instead of a comma rewrite, so they match Load's.
- plan's config reading (plan.Load, Validate, InspectConfig, and so bide check, bide view -config and bide edit) now refuses an identifier that holds a character that does not print: a control character, an invisible or direction-changing one (a zero-width space or joiner, a soft hyphen, the byte order mark, a bidirectional override such as U+202E), a space other than a plain one, a private-use or unassigned code point, any character with the Unicode property Default_Ignorable_Code_Point (the Hangul and Khmer fillers, the grapheme joiner, the Mongolian selectors, every variation selector, the zero-width joiner and non-joiner) or the Braille blank, a combining mark with nothing before it, or a space at either end. This also refuses some legitimate text, on purpose, because two names that differ only by an invisible character read the same and one can impersonate the other: a word spelled with a zero-width non-joiner (Persian, Urdu), an Indic conjunct spelled with a zero-width joiner, an emoji joined sequence (a family, a rainbow flag; a country flag, two regional indicators, is accepted), an emoji with a variation selector (a red heart), and a left-to-right or right-to-left mark. Transliterate the name or spell it without the joiner or selector. It applies to the flow name, entry, in and out, node names and blocks, every reference in edges, switches, arms and else, join names, inputs and merges, and the assert lines (whose words may still be separated by any whitespace). Such a name could read as another in a diff or a review, and was accepted and drawn as written. The error wraps agent.ErrConfig and names the field and the character written out, for example `nodes[2].name "ch\u{202E}gra" holds a character that does not print (U+202E)`. As a second line of defence bide check, bide check -diff, bide view, every page it serves and its error messages write any such character that reaches them (a file name, a journal's topology, a step or predicate name) as \u{XXXX}; its JSON endpoints and the other commands are not filtered. Upgrading: a config with such a name now fails to load with that error, which you can search your logs for; rename the node (or fix the block, flow or reference) to a name of printing characters and a plain space, everywhere the config uses it (edges, arms, assert lines). Renaming a node changes the topology digest, so a run paused under the old flow does not resume under the renamed one.
- The approve page draws from the shipped themes, inside its pinned bundle: light and dark by the system's color scheme, high-contrast when the system asks for more contrast (prefers-contrast: more), and the system's colors under forced colors, instead of two hand-written palettes that never saw a contrast check. The page takes no theme from a script, a URL, config.json or its host (a test holds that, and another holds the bundle's hashes in a committed golden, so a later change to a shipped theme is a visible change to the signed bundle). Text is 4.5:1, a control's edge 3:1, a disabled button has a dashed edge as well as its color, and the focus ring is drawn. REJECT now differs from Approve in shape (a heavy double edge against a thin solid one), in every mode: before this release the two differed by color and by their words only, and under forced colors, where both take the system's button colors, by their words alone. Upgrading: the approve page's SHA256SUMS and the Subresource Integrity hash in its index.html change in this release. Anyone who pins the approve page by hash, or hosts it on their own origin, must update the pin from this release's signed approve-page.SHA256SUMS (verify it as before) and redeploy the new bundle; a copy built earlier no longer matches. app.css is about 5 KB larger (1.5 KB to 6.9 KB); the script, the protocol and the page's behaviour are unchanged.
- States that bide view's graph and lists told apart by color alone now differ in shape or words as well, so they stay apart in greyscale, in forced colors and for a color-blind reader: a changed edge in the diff is dash-dot (an added one stays solid, a removed one a longer dash), a counterexample path is a heavy dotted line (the path taken stays a dash), a problem edge is a short dash, a warning node has a dashed box and a diamond mark where a problem has a circle, a row added by live mode carries a "new" badge, the run list's keyboard row has a marker and aria-current, and a stat's colored number has a glyph (check, cross, bang) on its label. A test now fails when two of these differ by color alone. A test in real Chrome (the Viewer (Chrome) job) draws each of these states from the real pages and fails if the browser computes two of them as the same line, and a fast static test guards the stylesheet text.
- bide view answers the system's accessibility settings. A reader whose system asks for more contrast (prefers-contrast: more) gets the high-contrast theme (black on white, 7:1 text), over a dark system setting and over -theme-default, unless they chose a theme in the page; when the system forces colors (forced-colors: active) the system's colors win over every theme, and the graph tells the run states apart by the shape of each node's box (thin solid done, dashes attempted, dash-dot waiting, heavy solid halted, dots not reached) as well as its glyph and words. The edge of every input, select and button now uses a new border_strong color that reaches 3:1 against its surroundings in every theme (it was a 1.4:1 hairline), and the states the pages draw in the info color (waiting for a person, the path taken, the last node, live) use a separate --info token, which has the accent's value in the shipped themes and which a theme file can set apart. The Viewer (Chrome) test now runs under all of these modes.
- The docs now describe the finished theme work: the themes guide, the design note's status, the roadmap and the docs index say that bide view, bide edit and the approve page draw from the checked themes and that a Go program can pass themes to planview and flowedit; KNOWN-LIMITATIONS says the colour and contrast checks cover the editor too and that the manual pass with a real contrast theme, macOS Increase contrast and a screen reader is still owed, and that forced colours do not dim the editor's save dialog; the editor design note's accessibility section describes the themes, the switch, the selection ring and the dialog.
- bide view draws from checked themes: its colours, fonts, spacing and radii come from one generated stylesheet (light, dark and a new high-contrast theme) instead of palettes written in the page's CSS, the header button and the t key step through the themes and name the current one, and bide view takes -theme file.json (a custom theme, which must pass bide theme check and is refused before anything is served if it does not) and -theme-default name. The shipped light and dark colours are retuned slightly so the status colours stay apart for a colour-blind reader and the idempotent step is no longer the same blue as a waiting gate (idempotent steps are now teal); the flow editor, which shares the viewer's stylesheet, takes the same colours.
- The flow editor guide shows a drag to connect in progress, and `scripts/shots.sh` regenerates the picture (`editor-structure`) with the others.

### Fixed

- Test only: the Go tests that drive a page in Chrome through a Node script (the editor, the run viewer and the approve page) ran the script with no deadline, so a Chrome or script that stalled held the test until the whole test binary timed out (one such run hung for 564 seconds with nothing said), and the approve page's client-certificate test, at its own limit, killed Node but left the Chrome it had started running. They now run the script under a five-minute deadline, kill it together with the Chrome it started, and fail with what it had printed. No behaviour of the product changes.
- `planview.CheckDiff` and `planview.DiffHandler` (a program's own diff check with its Registries; present since v0.15.0, reproduced on v0.21.0) passed a diff silently when a side had errors against its Registry: such a side has no gates to compare, so a gate removed by changing a block read as no change and `CheckDiff` returned false, where the same diff without Registries reports it. A common cause was one Registry shared by both versions that holds a block the config does not use (an error in `Load`). The diff is now made structurally, as without the Registry, so the weakening is reported as it would be without it; the result is labelled not certified (`DiffView.Fallback`), its verdict is never good, and `CheckDiff` returns true. Every result also says how each side was checked (`DiffView.OldBasis`, `NewBasis`). `bide check -diff` and `bide view -config -diff` pass no Registry, so they were never affected. To protect a pipeline that runs an earlier release, upgrade; until then give each version its own Registry, holding exactly the blocks that version's config uses (`plan.Load` requires it), so neither side has errors.
- bide edit's list edits kept only the items at the start and the end of a list, so an edit that replaced an item in the middle or that changed both ends deleted and re-wrote the items it meant to keep: the comments above or beside a kept item were lost, and a block list turned into a flow list. This affected an approval gate's approvers list (set_approval, from the first release of the editor) and the inputs of a join (set_join_inputs, new). A list edit now keeps the longest common subsequence of the old and the new list exactly as it is, with its comments and the list's style, inserts the new items where they belong and deletes only the old items that are not in the new list; removing one approver is one deleted item and adding one is one inserted item. If you saved a gate edit made with an earlier editor, check the diff of its approvers list for comments that went missing.
- `plan.InspectConfig` with a Registry no longer reports "Model step has no bound model" as an error for a config with a Model block (since v0.14.0, which added it). A check never runs the flow, so it does not need the model that `Load` binds. The programs affected are those that check a config against their own Registry: `planview.Handler`, `planview.Check`, `planview.DiffHandler`, `planview.CheckDiff` and direct `InspectConfig` callers; they showed such a config as broken, with no digest. `bide check` and `bide view -config` pass no Registry and were not affected.
- `bide check`, `bide view -config` and `plan.InspectConfig` without a Registry now make every check `plan.Load` and `plan.Build` make on the wiring alone, so a config that `bide check` passed could not still fail to load for a reason that needs no Go code. New: a cycle that is not a loop, a node not reached from the entry (worded so it reads as work in progress: a node you have declared and not wired in yet is a config that will not load), a node fed by several producers without a join, join inputs on mutually exclusive arms of a switch or that a switch can skip, and the shape of a loop's body (no inner switch or nested loop, no edge leaving or entering it past its head, no node between the head and the switch that is not on the loop path). They are Build's own checks run over a spec with no types, in Build's order; with a Registry nothing changes (Build makes them itself, after the type checks). Upgrading: a config with one of these now fails bide check, and the editor shows it as a config error until it is wired. The examples had one: examples/plan/flows/refund-policy-bypass.yaml, the policy-bypass demo that the run viewer guide, bide sample (flows/refund-bypass.yaml) and bide check -diff use, routed a vip arm straight to `charge`, which made `charge` fed by two producers, a config plan.Load has always refused; the earlier bypass example was a config Load refuses; it is now one that loads, with the bypass routed to an ungated node: the vip arm reaches a second charge step (`charge_vip`, block charge), so the violated assertion is now "every path to charge_vip passes approval >= 2 of 3" with the counterexample validate -> lookup -> charge_vip. A bypass of a gate on the same step cannot be a loading config, because a step with several producers needs a join.
- `bide check`, `bide view -config` and `plan.InspectConfig` without a Registry (the structural check) now report these things that `plan.Load` already refused and the structural check showed no error for, so a config that `bide check` passed could still fail to load: an edge that leaves a join, or a switch that is over a join, before the join's wiring element (a join is registered when its element is reached); an edge, an arm or an else into a join (a join is entered only by its inputs); a node that is switched over and has an edge out; two switches over one node; a join that reads a join defined after it in the wiring (Load registers a join when it reaches its wiring element, so the input is unknown; this includes a join that reads itself and a cycle of joins), a switch with more than one loop-back arm (an arm with loop_max above 0), a loop with no exit arm (no other arm and no else), and a loop-back arm whose target is not an ancestor of the switch in the forward graph (the edges, a join's inputs and the arms that do not loop back). Each is an error naming the wiring element. A config that bide check passed and Load refused for one of these now fails bide check too; fix it by defining the input join first, or by pointing the loop arm back at an earlier node of the loop. The rest of the wiring checks that Build makes, and the ones this list left out (a cycle that is not a loop, a node not reached from the entry, a node fed by several producers, the shape of a loop's body), are reported by the same check too since the structural check was completed later in this release; what a clean `bide check` still cannot show is what needs the program's Go side (names, types, gates a block declares), which `-blocks` covers.
- A named pipe given where bide expects a file no longer hangs it: `bide view` (`-sth`, `-evidence`, `-approver-keys`, `-stop-policy`, `-pubkey` as a file), `bide edit` (a config swapped for a pipe after its check), `bide approve` (`-item`, `-approver-keys`, `-cert`, `-log-key`, `-verify-receipt`, `-tls-ca`), `bide stop -stop-policy`, `bide notify` (`-secret-file`, `-previous-secret-file`, `-client-ca`, `-cert` and `-key`, `-ca`), the webhook notifier's client certificate, key and server CA files, `bide approve -tls-ca`, `-client-cert` and `-client-key`, `bide journal redact` (`-pubkey`, the JSON and signed-head files they read), `bide identity` and the identity preflight (`-policy`, `-params`, `-expect`, certificate chains, the files a policy names) and `bide-anchor -pubkey` open the file without waiting for a writer and refuse anything but a regular file, with the reason; a directory is refused the same way. **Upgrading:** these flags no longer accept a path that names a named pipe, a device or any other file that is not a regular file. A process substitution (`-pubkey <(vault read ...)`) still works on unix, as does `/dev/stdin` and a `/dev/fd/N` path (`/proc/self/fd/N` on Linux): the form is matched as written, so a symbolic link to one is refused, and the stream is read up to the flag's size limit. `/dev/stdin` is standard input and is owned like `-`. A flag that carries a secret or a policy also takes `-` for standard input, and one flag only can read it in a run (a second is refused, naming both): `-pubkey` (bide view, bide journal redact, bide-anchor), `-sth`, `-evidence`, `-approver-keys` and `-stop-policy` (bide view), `-stop-policy` (bide stop), `-item`, `-approver-keys` and `-log-key` (bide approve), and `-secret-file` and `-previous-secret-file` (bide notify); bide approve needs `-yes` with any of them, since the confirmation would read standard input too. A file named `-` is written `./-`. Files that had no size limit now have one, and a file over it is refused with a message naming the flag and the limit: 1 MiB for `-pubkey` (bide view, bide journal redact, bide-anchor) and `-log-key`; 16 MiB for `-verify-receipt`, `-tls-ca`, `-client-cert`, `-client-key`, the `bide notify` secret, CA, certificate and key files, `-params` (bide identity and the preflight), `-policy` and the files a policy names, `-expect`, and bide journal redact's `-sth` and `-authorization`; and the signed-heads log (`-head-out`, bide journal redact) has no total limit, which would stop a log that only grows: it is read one head per line, and a head over 64 KiB is refused (the heads are held whole in memory, as before, because the redaction trail checks and signs over the full chain of prior heads: about 0.3 to 1 KiB each, so a 260 MiB log of about 650,000 heads takes a few hundred MiB). The limits already in place are unchanged (`-item`, `-approver-keys`, `-stop-policy`, `-cert`, bide view's `-sth`, `-evidence` and journals). Errors that quote text from a document are bounded: the strict JSON reader quotes a field name escaped and cut to 64 characters, the decoding errors of `encoding/json` that pass through it are cut to 256 (theme, plan, tool-argument and audit documents), a theme's own messages quote a bad value the same way, a plan config's problem messages shorten each quoted name to 64 characters (the words around the names are kept) and `plan.Load`'s error is cut to 8 KiB, so a name or value as long as the file no longer makes a message as long as the file; a short name is still quoted whole, to help find the mistake. The library readers (`theme.Load` and the like) read regular files only, so what an error quotes comes from a file the caller could already read. Other messages of the audit and agent packages that quote verified fields are not cut.
- Test only: `TestNewTombstone_ReadsBack` built its changed-hash case by replacing the first two hex digits of the leaf hash with "00", which changed nothing when the hash already began with "00" (about one run in 256, since the record carries the time it was made), so the test failed at random. It now flips the first byte.
- Before release, `bide edit` against a Registry or a blocks description let an edit through that added a second problem of a kind the text already had: the config check (`plan.InspectConfig`, `plan.InspectConfigDescribed`) reported only the first type misfit, and the first of each wiring problem (a loop's shape, a node not reached from the entry, a join's routes), so the typed editor, which refuses an edit that adds a type problem or a loop-shape error by comparing the problems before and after, saw nothing new when a second one appeared. The check now reports every one of them: every edge, switch arm, join input and terminal whose types do not fit, up to 100 with a final line saying how many more there are, and every refusal of the wiring checks. `plan.Load` and `plan.Validate` list all the type problems in their error instead of the first. `bide check` and the viewer show the longer lists. Where there are several problems the first still leads, so a message or an error that began with it still does; `plan.Load` and `plan.Validate` errors, which name the first problem as before, now go on to name the others after it. The editor compares the complete lists of type problems, not the first 100 a report lists, so an edit cannot hide a new problem past that cap. The refusals of `plan.Load` and `plan.Validate` (type problems and the checks Build makes) now wrap `agent.ErrConfig`, so `errors.Is(err, agent.ErrConfig)` holds for them as it does for the other config errors; their text is unchanged. The editor identifies a problem by what it is about, taken from plan as structured records (class, kind, the full names, the types or levels), not by the text it shows, which shortens long names and names a wiring element by its position: so two problems whose names differ only past the 64th character are two, names with brackets or quotes cannot be confused, and moving an element does not make an old problem new. The type problems, the unsafe safeties and the loops' shape are found even when the config has another error (an unknown name, a safety above the registered one), so that error no longer hides them, and the editor compares them all (not the 100 a report lists). A safety stated above what the block registers is refused like a type problem. Building the topological order of a flow no longer scans every node for each node it frees, which made a flow of thousands of nodes slow to check.
- Test only: the two anchor/awss3 tests that check what a changelog entry says read CHANGELOG.md once a release has assembled the fragments, instead of skipping (the integration job fails on a skipped test).

### Security

- The strict JSON reader refuses a document whose structure nests more than 128 levels deep, and any document (JSON kept verbatim, such as a tool call's arguments or a tool's result in a journal record, included) that nests more than 10000, before any decoder runs, and no longer rebuilds the path to each value at every level. In v0.8.0 and later, `strictjson.Check` rebuilt a path string like `$[0][0]...` at each level, so its time and memory grew with the square of the nesting depth: 30 KB of `[` took 0.8 s and 2.1 GB under `encoding/json` v1 (GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2), 100 KB took 12 s and 30 GB, and 16 KB took about 160 MB in the default build. Every reader built on it was affected: agent tool-call arguments (input the model controls) and typed answers, `plan.Load`, `plan.Validate` and `plan.InspectConfig` (config loading), `audit.UnmarshalStrict` (signed tree heads, evidence and journal exports), and the stop-policy and approval-item readers (`bide stop`, `bide approve`) and theme files. A deeper document now fails with an error naming the limit and the offset (`nesting deeper than 128 levels at offset N`), at constant memory. Verbatim JSON is held to 10000, encoding/json's own limit and so the deepest a journal writer can record, so an export is never unreadable for what a model or tool put in a tool call or result. The deepest document bide itself reads or writes nests 5 levels (7 in the tests), so no valid input is affected; only a document whose structure is built to be deeper than 128 levels is refused. A very large flat document still costs memory in proportion to its size, and `plan.LoadReader` and the audit export readers do not cap it: see KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.

## [0.21.0] - 2026-10-19

### Added

- New module anchor/gcs: the second production audit.Anchor, which writes every signed tree head as an object in a Google Cloud Storage bucket whose retention policy is locked, with create-only writes, a read-back that refuses a retention, Cloud KMS key, hold or checksum weaker than configured, and a Reader that lists noncurrent generations so a deleted or overwritten newest head does not hide it, errors on two different signed heads of one size and on a well-formed head with a bad signature, and reports what it skipped.
