New refuses a bucket owned by another project, without a locked retention policy of at least the configured length, versioning, uniform bucket-level access, enforced public access prevention or the configured default key; the package ships least-privilege custom IAM roles (no delete, so no overwrite, and a separate hold-release identity), and builds on the new experimental audit/anchorobj. It was run once against a real bucket and is otherwise tested against an in-memory model; a locked policy cannot be undone, so read the [runbook](docs/testing/gcs-anchor-real-run.md) first, and a run or trail ID in an anchored head cannot be erased, so it must hold no personal data ([guide](docs/guides/anchoring-gcs.md)).
- New module anchor/awss3: the first production audit.Anchor, which writes every signed tree head as an object in an S3 bucket under Object Lock (compliance mode by default, governance only with AllowGovernance), reads it back and refuses a lock, retention or encryption weaker than configured, and keeps keys derived from the head so that the latest head of a trail and the head of any size are prefix listings with no mutable index; New refuses a bucket without Object Lock, versioning or a default retention, every request carries the expected bucket owner, writes are idempotent, and the package ships least-privilege IAM and bucket policies and a Reader and the bide-anchor command (verify, export, check) that verify the objects offline or with read-only credentials. It is tested against MinIO and a fake, never against AWS, and a run or trail ID in an anchored head cannot be erased, so it must hold no personal data ([anchoring guide](docs/guides/anchoring.md)).
- `bide approve -item FILE` (and `bide-hsm approve -item`) takes the run and the call from one call of `bide approve -list -json` output, a file or `-` for standard input (which also needs `-yes`), instead of `-run` and `-call`. The input is the call's object or an array of exactly one, decoded strictly (no repeated name, no invalid UTF-8), and the ids are used exactly as decoded, so an id that ends in a line break or holds a bidirectional or invisible character, which a pasted command line or a shell's `$(...)` cannot carry, can be decided from the command line. `-item` with `-run`, `-call`, `-list` or `-verify-receipt` is a usage error, and the input is limited to 1 MiB. The fallback texts of `bide view` and `bide approve -list` for such ids, the run viewer and approval guides and the CLI references point to it.
- `bide audit redactions` now says how a run ended when its end marker was erased: for each run whose erased end marker the signed redaction trail accounts for (name, leaf hash and time all match), it prints `run r1: ended: completed (per trail)` (or aborted, or cancelled), and `audit.RedactionReport.Ended` carries the run, the marker the trail recorded and the kind. The journal itself says only that such a run is over (`agent.RunEnded`), because a tombstone's name is outside the leaf hash and can be renamed with no key; the signed trail is the one source of the kind. A tombstone renamed to another end marker is still `NAME_MISMATCH` and reports no kind, and a run whose end marker is kept reports none.
- audit.CertIdentity now reports the certificate rules a request for a certificate must ask for: IDKind, EKU, AllowsEmailProtectionEKU, PolicyOID and MaxLeafLifetime (the effective value, 24 hours when the policy set none). They are read-only (EKU returns a copy) and change no verdict; they let a tool such as a certificate request command name what the gate will look for.
- `bide-hsm csr -key <ref>` writes a PKCS#10 certificate signing request for a key held in an HSM or a cloud KMS, signed by that key through the same key sources as `bide-hsm approve` (PKCS#11, AWS KMS, Google Cloud KMS, Azure Key Vault; Ed25519 or ECDSA P-256 as the source has them), so an approver no longer needs the vendor's tooling to get a certificate for it. The request names nothing by default; `-san-email`, `-san-uri` and `-eku` ask for a SAN and a usage, and `-policy <file> -as <approver>` asks for exactly what the gate's identity policy looks for and prints what the CA must issue. Before it writes, it checks the request's signature, that its public key is the key source's, and that it carries only the names asked for; it writes to `-out` or standard output and never overwrites a file without `-force`. `bide keygen -csr` now builds its request through the same code. Ed25519 and P-256 only: there is no RSA request.
- `audit.CertApprover.CheckCertificate` returns the verdict a gate reaches on a decision's certificate chain without a journal, signature or signed head: whether the chain is accepted for the approver and what the revocation status rule makes of it, judged by the counting rule (`audit.CheckCount`) or the pre-execution rule (`audit.CheckExec`) at a given decision and provable time. `VerifyCertDecision` and `VerifyCertExec` now call the same function for that part, so there is no second copy of the verdict; their behavior is unchanged, and a test pins the two to the same answer across the identity tests.
- A control mapping page, `docs/trust/control-mapping.md`, for bank risk and procurement reviews: for SOC 2 (CC6 to CC8), ISO/IEC 27001:2022 Annex A, PCI DSS v4.0 requirement 10, DORA, the EU AI Act (Articles 12, 14 and 15) and SR 11-7 / OCC 2011-12, it lists what bide provides with links to the guide or code that shows it, what the institution must still provide, and what is not covered yet (a production anchor and a kill switch in progress, independent time not used by any gate, no external security review). It is not a certification or legal advice and says an assessor decides. It is linked from the docs index and the docs-site Trust sidebar.
- `bide keygen -csr` and `bide-hsm csr` take `-cn <name>`, which makes the request's subject one common name (printable ASCII text of at most 64 characters, no leading or trailing space; a bad name is a usage error before any key is used or any billed signature made). The gate reads no subject, so the name changes nothing about who may approve; it exists for tools such as `step ca sign` that refuse a request with an empty subject and take the certificate's content from the CA's template. Without `-cn` the request is unchanged: an empty subject.
- A legal hold's lift can need a second person: `bide journal hold authorize` has a second person sign, with a key of their own (a key file, or with the new `bide-hsm journal` a key in an HSM or KMS), their consent to lift one hold, bound to the hold as it stood (matter, runs, tree setting), the placement's trail position and leaf hash, the deployment and a validity; `bide journal hold lift -authorization FILE -authorizer-pubkey KEY -require-authorization` checks it before anything is written and embeds it in the lift's signed trail record (`audit.LiftLegalHold` takes `audit.WithLiftAuthorization`, `WithLiftAuthorizers` and `RequireLiftAuthorization`; `audit.NewLiftAuthorization` and `audit.LiftAuthorization` make and carry it; a refusal is `audit.ErrLiftUnauthorized`). The authorizer's key must not be the key that signs the trail or share a component with it (a hybrid key's Ed25519 half is the same key holder). `bide audit redactions` checks the embedded authorization offline: a bad signature, another hold or placement, a validity lapsed at the lift, the lifter's own key, or a key that is not one of the `-authorizer-pubkey` keys is the new finding `LIFT_UNAUTHORIZED`, and `-require-lift-authorization` makes a lift with none one too. `bide-hsm journal` is `bide journal` with its signing keys named by KMS or PKCS#11 reference. Placing a hold needs no second person.
- `bide identity check -cert` reports `cert.cdp`: with CRLs loaded it fails when the issuer's CRLs carry an issuing distribution point (what step-ca stamps) that none of the leaf's plain CRL distribution points names, which is why no CRL covers the certificate and the gate waits, and it names the fix; it warns when the leaf has several plain distribution points with different names, since status is then read from a CRL covering any one of them, which suits mirrors of one CRL and not a CA that partitions. `audit.PlainDistributionPoints` returns a certificate's plain distribution points, the ones a CRL's issuing distribution point is matched against.
- `bide identity check` is the pre-flight for a certificate-bound approval setup, for a bank to run before go-live and an approver to run when a decision is refused. It reports one line per check, with the fix, or the same as JSON (`-json`): the policy file loads and its pinned CAs and intermediates are valid and match their certificates; the declared CA timings pass the gate's own configuration check; the policy file and `-params` agree member by member with the policy record the gate journals (`-expect`), with the members `-params` cannot declare listed as not verified; the CRLs, read offline from a `fetch-crl` archive or a flat directory (`-crl-dir`, never written) or fetched with the refresher's own limits (`-fetch`), are fresh by the refresher's health model and the status rule's freshness test; and, for an approver's chain (`-cert`), whether the gate would accept it and let the call run, with the gate's own reason if not. It never reports ready while a part was left out: `-no-status` and `-no-expect` give a partial result (exit 4), a failed check exits 1, a usage error 2, an unreadable file 3. Every report lists what a pre-flight cannot check ahead of time. The chain verdict is `audit.CertApprover.CheckCertificate`, the function the gate's counting and execution checks call.
- Added the signed acknowledgement of a planted redaction-trail record: `audit.AcknowledgeRecord` and `bide journal acknowledge -position N -reason ... -ticket ... -deployment ... -sign-key ... -head-out ...` append a record, signed with the redaction key and bound to its position, that names a leaf the tool did not write by its position and the hash of its bytes, with the reason, the ticket and who, and sign a new head. `bide audit redactions` then reports that leaf as acknowledged (a note, printed as INFO, that says who acknowledged it, the ticket, the time and the reason) instead of failing on its BAD_SIGNATURE, REORDERED or MALFORMED. It acknowledges only that leaf: the acknowledgement must come after it, a record the tool wrote or an erasure made by hand cannot be acknowledged, other bytes at that position fail again, and an unsigned or wrongly signed acknowledgement acknowledges nothing. `audit.CheckRedactionTrail` is the check `RedactJournal` makes before it appends, for the command.
- Added `bide journal redact`, the command that erases personal data from finished runs accountably, with `agent.Compactor` (implemented by `store/sqlite`) behind its storage step. It lists a plan as a dry run (each record by position, key, kind, length and leaf hash, never content, with a digest), and with `-execute -plan-digest` erases the records of a finished run into tombstones: it refuses a run that is not over, is leased (checked before anything is written), has a live parent, belongs to a session or has events in the event store (`-keep-event-copies` is recorded), refuses a run no verified signed journal head covers unless `-allow-uncovered` (recorded), and refuses a trail that does not match the heads in `-head-out` (a typo in `-trail-db`) or that holds records no earlier head attests (unless `-accept-unheaded-trail`). Every trail record is signed and bound to the deployment named by the required `-deployment`; the trail's head is signed with a key file and appended to `-head-out`, also after a failed pass. On SQLite the erasure runs with `secure_delete` on and the command then checkpoints and truncates the write-ahead log; when a reader holds the database open it says "STORAGE NOT COMPACTED" and exits 1 rather than claim success, and a test reads the database and WAL files for the erased bytes with another connection open. On Postgres the erasing role keeps its own DSN (`-redact-postgres-env`) because it can read nothing, the replaced row versions stay until the operator's `VACUUM` (the report says so), and an end-to-end test erases through that role and passes `bide audit redactions`. The trail is signed from the command line with a key file; HSM and KMS signing go through the library until `bide-hsm` gains it. The report ends with the copies the command cannot reach. There is no legal hold or second-person authorization yet.
- Added the optional `agent.Redactor` store capability, implemented by `MemStore`, `store/sqlite` and `store/postgres`, which replaces one entry of a finished run with a redaction tombstone that keeps its position and audit leaf hash, together with `agent.PlanRedaction` (a dry run that lists records by position, name and leaf hash and never their content, with a digest), `agent.Redact`, `agent.NewTombstone` (a tombstone is read only in the exact byte form it is written, and now carries an optional `name` member, always written, that must equal the row it sits in, so `audit.JournalExport.Journal()` recovers the name of a redacted record and decision coverage from an export still lists a redacted approval), `agent.TombstoneName`, `agent.LeafHash`, `agent.CheckRedaction`, `agent.RedactionProtected` and `storetest.Redactor`, the conformance suite. A store refuses, writing nothing, a run with no end marker, a run with a live lease, a sub-agent's run while a run above it is not over, a session's runs, a protected record (the journal header, end markers, attempt markers, saga failures the saga compensation records and the records an approval count rests on), a tombstone that does not keep the leaf hash of the bytes it replaces (the store recomputes it), and an entry that changed since the caller read it. The core `Store` port is unchanged. `agent.Status` now reports a run whose `run:start` was redacted as started rather than not started. On Postgres the migration (run as the schema owner) creates a `SECURITY DEFINER` function, `bide_redact_v3`, that repeats every check server-side and builds the tombstone; the erasing role holds `EXECUTE` on it and no privilege on any table, so the application's role cannot erase and raw SQL by the erasing role cannot rewrite a record. A tombstone at a name a redaction never replaces is reported as corruption (`ErrStorage`) by every reader, and a tombstone's name, which is outside the leaf hash, is labelled "not attested" where a reader acts on it (`audit.NoteRedacted` now reads "redacted, name not attested"; `agent.RunStatus.StartRedacted`). There is no command yet and the erasure is not yet recorded in an audit trail; see the retention guide's section on erasing personal data for what it does not reach.
- Added `bide journal hold place|lift|list` and the signed record of legal holds: `audit.PlaceLegalHold` places a hold in the store and then records it in the redaction trail (signed with the redaction key, bound to its position, with the matter, the runs, the reason, the ticket and who), and `audit.LiftLegalHold` records the lift first and then lifts the hold, so a hold is never lifted without evidence and a failure to record never leaves a run unprotected. `bide journal redact` refuses a held run and its dry run names the hold. `bide audit redactions` lists the holds the trail shows in force, compares them with the database's (`HOLD_NOT_IN_STORE` for one lifted by hand, `HOLD_NOT_IN_TRAIL` for one placed by hand, `HOLD_DIFFERS`: other runs, tree setting or matter), reports a lift or placement the trail does not match (`HOLD_UNMATCHED`), and reports an erasure that began while the trail showed a hold over the run (`REDACTED_UNDER_HOLD`: when the pass replaced records, or has no end record to say it did not); on Postgres the reading role needs `SELECT` on the holds table. Two operators placing or lifting the same hold at once leave one record, not a duplicate that the audit would report as `HOLD_UNMATCHED`. Placing a hold again records one whose record failed, and lifting again finishes a lift whose store step failed, with no second record; a hold lifted by hand can be recorded as lifted.
- Added legal holds at the store: the optional `agent.Holder` capability (`PlaceHold`, `LiftHold`, `Holds`; `agent.Hold` names an ID, a matter, the runs it covers and whether it covers the sub-agent runs below them), implemented by `MemStore`, `store/sqlite` and `store/postgres`, with `storetest.Holder` as its conformance suite. A store's redaction now refuses a run an active hold covers (`agent.ErrRedactHeld`) in the same statement that would replace the record, so a hold placed while a pass runs either lands first or the record was already erased; `agent.PlanRedaction` names the holds that cover a run and refuses it (`RedactionPlan.Holds`). SQLite and Postgres keep the holds in a `bide_holds` table the migration creates (a file or schema from before holds has none and no hold). On Postgres the redaction function moves to `bide_redact_v3`, which reads the table as its owner, so the erasing role can neither read nor change it and a third role with `SELECT, INSERT, DELETE` on that one table places and lifts holds; the migration creates `v3` beside any older `bide_redact_v1` or `v2` (`v1` does not check holds), and revokes `EXECUTE` on every older version from PUBLIC and every role it was granted to (it never replaces or drops a function), and the store calls only `v3`, telling you to run the migration when it is missing. `v3` takes the holds table's `SHARE` lock first, so a placed hold stops every erasure that has not yet committed, and refuses to run unless its transaction is at `READ COMMITTED`: a role whose default isolation is stricter needs `ALTER ROLE ... SET default_transaction_isolation = 'read committed'`. There is no command for holds and no record of them in the signed redaction trail yet.
- audit.AuditedStore.WithMinInterval and audit.AuditedEventStore.WithMinInterval publish at most one head per run or trail per interval, so an anchor that keeps every head for years (an S3 Object Lock bucket, say) no longer receives one per write: growth inside the interval is held back and published as one covering head by a timer, so while the process lives every record is covered within the interval, and a run's first head, its end marker (run:complete or run:aborted), Reanchor, Flush, ReanchorRuns and, for trails, ReanchorPending and AnchorTrails publish at once. A process that dies loses its timers, and what it held back stays unanchored until the next start or recovery pass: AuditedStore.ReanchorRuns is that pass for journals, and AnchorTrails already is for trails. The new AuditedStore.Flush and Pending, AuditedEventStore.Flush (which now also publishes held-back trails) and the default of no interval leave existing behaviour unchanged. A failed publish by the timer is retried with a doubling wait and reported to OnError once per streak, with the new OnRecovered hook ending it, and bide audit verify-decision-coverage and verify-receipts now say which of a journal's last records are under no signed head, and verify-decision-coverage takes -max-unanchored to fail on a tail whose newest head is older than a bound.
- The `-params` file of `bide identity fetch-crl` and `bide identity check` takes optional members for the values the gate's policy digest covers and a declaration could not state before: `w`, `min_w`, `routine_grace`, `clock_tolerance`, `allow_holds`, `consecutive_numbers`, `retained` and `effective_from` (named as in the policy record), beside the existing `coverage_tolerance`. A file that states all of them builds an approver whose policy digest equals the gate's for the same declaration (`audit.StatusDecl.MissingForDigest` names what a file lacks), so the digest `fetch-crl` prints in its start-up events is comparable, and `bide identity check -expect-digest` is a true equality; with a file that lacks some, `fetch-crl` says which and that the digest cannot be compared, and `check` fails naming them. Existing files parse and behave as before, and unknown members are still refused.
- Added the redaction trail and its check: `audit.RedactJournal` erases the records of a plan accountably (it requires a reason, a ticket, an executor, a time and a signer, and signs every trail record with it, bound to its position, the hash of the record before it and a required deployment name (a replayed, copied or reordered record is `REORDERED`), so a record planted by someone who can append to the event store is `BAD_SIGNATURE` (or `MALFORMED`) under any head and accounts for nothing; `audit.RedactJournal` also refuses (`ErrRedactTrailUntrusted`) before it appends or erases when the trail does not match `RedactionOptions.TrailHeads`, but a record planted in the trail never blocks an erasure: it is reported and accounts for nothing; `audit.SignRedactionTrail` verifies the earlier heads before it signs a head over exactly the leaves it checked; refuses a run no verified signed journal head covers unless `AllowUncovered`, which is recorded; appends a begin record before it erases and an end record after, to a trail named `redact:@log` in an event store that `audit.AuditedEventStore` or `audit.SignNotifyTrail` can sign and anchor), `audit.VerifyRedactions` and the new `bide audit redactions` verb. The check finds a deleted or changed trail record, records after the newest head, an unfinished pass, a tombstone no trail record names (an erasure made by hand), a tombstone whose name or leaf hash differs from the trail's (a tombstone's name is outside the journal's hashes, so the signed trail is what attests it), a record the trail lists that still holds content, a tombstone stamped at a time no pass that listed it carries, a run the trail names that has no journal in the input (a trail checked against no journal checked nothing), and an erasure that ran without a covering head; a tombstone is accounted for only by the pass that erased it (a later plan that lists it as already redacted does not launder an erasure made by hand), and a failed pass that the same plan later finished is a note. `-trail-db` and `-trail-postgres-env` read the trail from a store kept apart from the journal. `agent.RedactUsing` lets a different store than the journal's do the replacing. `audit.SignRedactionTrail` and `bide audit redactions` take the deployment name too (`-deployment` is required). There is no command to erase yet.
- Releases now carry SLSA v1 Build Level 3 build provenance: the release workflow calls a reusable workflow of a separate repository (`bide-ai/release-builder`, pinned by commit) that builds with goreleaser and attests every archive, every archive SBOM, `checksums.txt`, `approve-page.SHA256SUMS` and the module set SBOM with GitHub's artifact attestations, and `release.yml` verifies that provenance with a pinned `gh` (`scripts/verify-provenance.sh`) before it signs or publishes anything, failing the release if one file does not verify. The cosign signature identity is unchanged. `docs/trust/verify-releases.md` has the `gh attestation verify` commands and states what the provenance proves and what it does not.
- `docs/guides/sso-certificates.md` and the `examples/sso-certs` module: a worked, tested recipe for short-lived approver certificates issued after a single sign-on login, with step-ca 0.30.2 as the reference CA (an OIDC provisioner and a certificate template that issue exactly what an `IdentityPolicy` requires, the lifetime and backdate rule, an operator JWK provisioner for revocation) and `scripts/renew.sh` for the approver. The module's test runs the recipe against pinned step-ca, step CLI and mock OIDC identity provider containers: the real login, the certificate's shape, a login without MFA or with a malformed person id refused by the template, `bide identity fetch-crl` and `bide identity check` over step-ca's CRL, a gated call approved with `bide approve -cert` and counted by a real `audit.CertApprover`, a revocation and the refused decision made after it. It runs in the Integration job with `BIDE_REQUIRE_STEPCA=1` and elsewhere only with `BIDE_TEST_STEPCA=1`; the guide states what the test does not cover (an HSM or KMS key, another identity provider, other CAs).
- `bide audit notifications -db` (and `-postgres-env`) checks the kill switch's notifications from the control run: every stop in force must have been told (`stop_set`), whatever its recorded time says (a record's time is its writer's own, so a backdated or future-dated stop is still owed; `-stops-within`, default 7 days and the notifier's window, limits only stops that are over, and `NotifyPending` now notifies every stop in force the same way), a lifted stop owes its all-clear and its `stop_lifted`, and a stop set and lifted before any notifier looked owes only the lift; a stop or lift no notifier saw fails with `never_notified`, and a control run the audit cannot judge (signed records and no `-stop-policy`) is refused with exit 4 instead of passing what it did not check. `-stop-policy` and `-stop-root` read the control run as the deployment pins its policy. `audit.VerifyNotifications` takes the control run's state as `NotifyVerifyInput.Stops` (judged with `agent.StopNotifyEvents`, which shares its window rule with the notifier) and reports a control run record dated in the future, or well before an earlier record, as a problem (`StopAnomalies`, `agent.StopClockAnomalies`); with `-run` the command prints an INFO line that stops were not checked and now reads the `stop_set` and `stop_lifted` kinds, the all-clear reason `lifted` and the stop key shapes, which it refused as unknown before (`agent.ParseStopKey`). Guide: docs/guides/notifications.md.
- `bide stop`, the kill switch's command line (and `bide-hsm stop` for a key in an HSM or a KMS): `-all`, `-tool`, `-flow` and `-agent` set a stop (there is no default scope, and a bare `bide stop` lists the active stops and exits 2), `-list` (with `-json`), `-show`, `-lift`, `-reaudit`, `-apply-pending`, `-upgrade` and `-downgrade` work on it. Basic mode needs no keys; audited mode (`-stop-policy`, `-as`, `-key`) signs each record with an enrolled approver's key, checks the key and role before writing, and lifts by the policy's quorum. A stop is written without a prompt when `-yes` is given or stdin is not a terminal; a lift and the configuration operations ask on a terminal and refuse without one unless `-yes`. Exit statuses are 0, 1, 2, 3 and 5, where 5 means an audited record landed in the control run or on the trail but not both (`-reaudit` and `-apply-pending` complete it). No secret goes on the command line: a key is a file or a key reference, a Postgres journal is named by its DSN variable. `bide audit stops` gains `-sign-head -log-key -head-out`, a schedulable job that verifies the stop trail against the control run, signs a head over exactly the verified records (`audit.SignStopTrailExport`) and appends it to a head file that `-sth` reads; a deployment must run it, or the audit fails on the unattested tail. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- The control run of the kill switch (`agent.StopControl`, `StopPolicy`, `StopRecord`): stop, lift, policy-transition and mode records under the reserved run ID `@stop`, which a recovery pass does not drive and no user run may use. The mode is each process's configuration: basic (no policy, the default) trusts the store, so anyone with store access sets and lifts and the records are plain; audited (a policy) counts only records signed by approvers of the policy in force at their position, lifts by a quorum of distinct lifters (one not the setter, configurable) and ignores plain and unsigned records. No store record can switch a process's mode or make it fail: mode records are evidence, and `StopState` lists what it rejected. The deployment's root policy, which carries a deployment ID that every signed record repeats (so records do not replay across deployments), is the trust anchor, and signed policy transitions approved by a quorum of the policy in force carry later ones; a process pins the root policy (or a later one, naming the root it trusts with `WithStopRoot`) and walks forward from that root only, so a self-signed root a store writer appends never governs a record, so a rotation or an offboarding never drops a stop, and a chain it cannot walk (a pin no stored root reaches, a fork) makes the stops unknowable instead of silently dropping them, while a forged record in an audited deployment is only rejected; a basic process on an audited deployment does not lift a signed stop on a plain lift; `ChangeMode` to audited adopts the stops that are active in the basic view. every process declares its mode (`WithStopMode`), the chain is checked for forks and rollbacks (`ErrConfig`), and a control that cannot judge which stops are in force (a basic process on an audited deployment, an audited process whose pin the chain does not reach) reports `ErrStopsUnknowable` for the engine to fail closed on; `StopControl.State` folds the log into the effective stops; nothing in the engine reads it yet, so no run is stopped by this change. Model 16 (`spec/tla/stop`) checks the fold and a call's read of it, with eight counterexample configurations.
- The kill switch notifies: a stop that is set (`stop_set`, key `stop:<id>`) and a stop that is lifted (`stop_lifted`, key `stop:<id>:lifted`) are notification kinds, observed from the control run so delivery has the notify trail's claim, acknowledgement, retry, overdue and escalation and survives the death of the process that set the stop. `NotifyPending` given `WithNotifyStops(ctl)` reads the control run on each sweep (stops set or lifted within `WithNotifyStopsWithin`, default 7 days), and `NewStopNotifier` with `WithStopObserver` on a `StopSwitch` notifies within the switch's poll interval; both name an event by its key, so any number of processes and both paths send one notification. The observer is handed each change through a single latest-state slot on a goroutine of its own, so a blocked or panicking observer or notifier never delays or fails a check point. A `stop_set` ends with the usual all-clear (reason `lifted`), an audited stop's `stop_lifted` is raised once when the quorum lands, a rejected record raises nothing, and routing and escalation apply. The webhook payload gains `stop` (id, scope, name, reason, setter, host, mode, signed, compensation, lifted_by; no key, signature or secret), the Slack relay escapes the operator text, PagerDuty sends `stop_set` as critical and `stop_lifted` as info, and `bide notify test -kind stop_set` and `stop_lifted` send samples. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- The kill switch's engine side (`agent.StopSwitch`, `agent.WithStopSwitch`, `*agent.Stopped`, `agent.WithRecoverStop`): an agent given a switch holds a covered run, as a pause, at a drive's start, at a turn's start, before a call's claim, after the claim and immediately before the call begins, so no side effect starts under a stop that was committed before the check; a won claim that is held is recorded as not started, a call that has begun finishes and is journaled, a written attempt marker is never fired again by a stop or its lift, and a saga's rollback continues unless the stop blocks compensation. A side-effect call reads the control run itself and is held (fail closed) when it cannot be read past a staleness bound, or when the stops cannot be judged; Recover and RecoverLoop list nothing during a global stop. An agent without a switch on a store whose control run holds a record fails its drives with `ErrConfig`, which costs one extra Load per store every few seconds on a drive (the budget tests count it). Model 16 maps the check points. A plan flow takes a switch with `Builder.WithStopSwitch` (or `plan.WithLoadedStopSwitch`): a flow-scope stop holds a run at its start and each node before its claim and after it, with the same not-started record, and a flow without a switch on such a store is refused. A multi-handle Postgres test shows a stop set by one process holds another's next side-effect call. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- The kill switch's audit trail: an audited `agent.StopControl` given `WithStopTrail(audit.NewStopTrail(events))` writes every signed record (a stop, a lift, a policy transition, the root, a mode change) to a stop trail of salted event leaves that an `AuditedEventStore` signs and anchors, in an order that keeps the evidence at least as strong as the effect (a stop is in force first and evidenced second; every other record is evidenced first), reports a failed trail write as `*agent.StopTrailError`, and `StopControl.Reaudit` appends what the trail lacks. `bide audit stops` verifies a trail offline (`-trail`, or `-db` and `-postgres-env`) against the pinned policy (`-stop-policy`) and the chain of signed transitions from its root, against signed heads (`-sth`, `-pubkey`) so a deleted or reordered record is found, and, with a store, against the control run record for record. `audit.VerifyStops`, `audit.ExportStopTrail`, `audit.SignStopTrail` and `agent.VerifyStopRecords` are the library side. A trail record that never reached the control run (a crash between the two writes) is listed as unapplied, and `StopControl.PendingRecords` lists it with its signer and age and `StopControl.ApplyPending` applies it, as a signed `apply` record naming what was applied and by whom; an unattested tail fails by default and `-max-unattested` relaxes it. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- `bide view` shows the kill switch: while a stop is in force every page of a store opens with a banner (scope, reason, setter, age, lifts held and needed, the mode), and the banner also appears when the stops cannot be judged, signed records cannot be checked, records were rejected, or the control run cannot be read, so a page never says "no stop" about a control run it could not read. `-stop-policy` and `-stop-root` check the signatures as an audited deployment does; without them the banner says signatures are not verified. The run list and a run's page say which runs a stop holds (a global stop, a flow stop, a tool stop with a call the journal shows no result for; an agent stop only "may be held", since the agent identity is not journaled), `/stops.json` serves the banner (with `read_at`; a viewer given the policy on a deployment that has not upgraded says its plain stops are ignored by audited processes), and `bide stop -lift` shows how many runs the stop holds before it asks. The control run `@stop` is no longer listed as a run. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- `bide-hsm audit stops -sign-head -log-key <key ref> -head-out <file>` signs the kill switch's stop-trail head with a log key held in an HSM or a cloud KMS (a `pkcs11:` URI, `awskms:`, `gcpkms:` or `azurekv:` reference), closing the gap that `bide audit stops -sign-head` took a key file only. It is the same verb as `bide audit stops` (one implementation in `internal/auditcli`): it signs only the records it verified against the pinned policy and the control run, signs nothing when the audit is not clean, adds nothing when the newest head already commits to every record, opens the key only when there is something to sign and only after checking it is the key `-pubkey` names, and has the same exit statuses; the heads it writes verify with the existing verifier. The reference holds no secret (a PKCS#11 PIN comes from `pin-source`), and the key flags of `bide-hsm approve` apply. `bide-hsm audit` runs the `stops` verb only. Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.

### Changed

- New package audit/anchorobj (experimental): the SDK-free half of an object-store audit anchor, extracted from anchor/awss3 so that the Google Cloud Storage anchor and any later one name, encode and check anchored heads the same way: the object layout (partition hash, inverted size, head digest), the canonical object body and the checks a Reader makes on it, the equivocation check and the tripwire for run IDs that look like personal data. A backend supplies its own object format and partition tag, so an object of one anchor never validates as another's, and its own error values, so errors.Is and the text of its messages are unchanged. anchor/awss3 now uses it with no change in behaviour: the existing tests pass unchanged and new tests pin the object keys, bodies and partition names byte for byte.
- The `bide check` warning for a quoted `assert` name that contains a backslash sequence now reads as guidance: it says the name is read as written, names the node the line refers to now (the name with its backslash) and says to write the character itself if that was meant, instead of describing how the old reading would have unescaped it. The condition that triggers it is unchanged.
- The bide-anchor command moves out of the anchor/awss3 module into its own module, github.com/bide-ai/bide/cmd/bide-anchor (install it with go install github.com/bide-ai/bide/cmd/bide-anchor@latest), and gains -backend, which names the object store the anchor wrote to; s3, the default, is the only backend built in, and others are added with their packages. It has not been in a release, so no published install path changes; the module is tagged with the others from the next release.
- Added docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, a "Reading the code" map of the source: for each main package (agent, audit, plan, approvals, notify, the stores, govern, flowedit and cmd/bide) the files to open first, in reading order, starting with the journal, step, claims and leases, the loop, approvals and the exec gate, the notifier and the audit log. The agent, audit and plan package docs now carry a "Where to start" paragraph, and the README, the docs index and the docs site link the page. Docs only; no code changed.
- A CRL whose critical issuing distribution point holds only a full name made of URIs (the shape step-ca stamps on every CRL it signs) is now read instead of refused. Because such a point narrows the CRL's scope (RFC 5280 section 5.2.5), it is evidence of absence only for a certificate whose own CRL distribution points name one of its URIs byte for byte, in a point with no reasons and no cRLIssuer; a certificate that does not name it gets no status from it and the gate waits, as before. A listing in any CRL the issuer signed still revokes, whatever its point. The CRL refresher refuses (an `unsupported-crl` event naming both URLs) a CRL whose point does not name the URL it was fetched from, and `audit.CRL` gains `DistributionPoints`. Every other issuing distribution point (a scope flag, a reasons field, a relative name, a name that is not a URI, a non-critical one) and any delta CRL with one are refused as before.
- The kill switch, redaction and legal holds are labelled experimental in the API reference and the stability page: every symbol of `agent` and `audit` declared for them (`StopControl`, `StopSwitch`, the stop records, policy and trail, `Redactor`, `Compactor`, `PlanRedaction`, `Redact`, the redaction trail, `Holder`, `PlaceLegalHold`, `LiftLegalHold` and the rest) now carries *(experimental)* through the label table's symbol rules (`internal/tools/apiref/labels.go`), and the kill switch and retention guides and `docs/trust/stability.md` say what it means: the Go API and the command flags may change in a minor release with a changelog entry, their records follow the pre-1.0 format policy of the stability page, the tombstone format and leaf hash stay core, and the stated guarantees (a stop in force holds the calls it covers, an erasure is accounted for in a signed trail) hold. No code behavior changes.
- A redaction can now erase an end marker (`run:complete`, `run:aborted`, `run:cancelled`), by naming it (`bide journal redact -name run:complete`), so the run's final answer, or its cancellation or failure text, can be erased. `-payloads` does not select an end marker, and its dry run says so (`end marker not selected by the Payloads preset: name run:complete to erase its content`), so nothing is left behind silently. The tombstone keeps the marker's position, and every reader finds the run over by it, but none reads how the run ended from it, because a tombstone's name is outside the leaf hash and a rename would flip a completed run to cancelled: `agent.Status` reports the new state `agent.RunEnded` with `agent.RunStatus.EndRedacted` set and no `Terminal` text, `IsComplete` is false, `Cancel` reports `ErrRunEnded`, `Recover`, `RecoverLoop` and a drive treat the run as over and refuse to drive it, the notifier does not raise an abort from an erased `run:aborted`, `bide view` shows `ENDED (ERASED)`, and a flow's `Conform` does not compare an erased completion. Only the signed redaction trail records what the marker was (`bide audit redactions`, `NAME_MISMATCH` for a rename). `agent.RedactionProtected` no longer lists the end markers; a tombstone at any other `run:` name is still corruption. On Postgres the redaction function moves to `bide_redact_v3`, which allows the end markers; the migration creates it and revokes `EXECUTE` on every older `bide_redact_vN` from PUBLIC and every grantee, so only the current function is callable, and you grant the erasing role `EXECUTE` on `v3` (the store calls only `v3`). The lifecycle model gained the erasure (`RedEnd`, model 10): `life-redact` passes, `regress/redacted-not-end` and `regress/redacted-kind-from-name` show the two bugs (reading an end by content, reading its kind from the name).
- The release workflow handles pre-release tags (`vX.Y.Z-rc.N`): the module set SBOM accepts a semantic version with a pre-release suffix (no leading zeros, no empty identifiers, checked the same way by `scripts/module-sbom.sh` and the release builder), the release is marked a pre-release as before, and no Homebrew formula is generated or pushed for a tag with a hyphen, so `brew` never offers a pre-release as the current version.
- `scripts/release.sh --push` now waits for main's CI instead of failing at once when the release commit has no completed push run yet (right after an admin merge the run has often not started): it polls every 30 seconds (`BIDE_CI_PREFLIGHT_POLL_SECS`) for up to 45 minutes (`BIDE_CI_PREFLIGHT_WAIT_SECS`) with a progress line, stops at once on a failure, and still accepts a passed merge queue run of the same tree. A dry run asks once. The self-test covers waiting, failure, the time limit and the dry run.
- A writer with store write access cannot stop a kill switch stop from landing, but can slow it: every full read of the control run folds every entry, so `SetStop`, `State()` and a `StopSwitch`'s first read grow linearly (a switch's later reads are incremental and stay cheap until new entries arrive) with the junk entries a writer adds under `@stop/set/` (measured, one global `SetStop` with 100,000 junk entries: 0.84 s on SQLite, 2.0 s on Postgres; with 10,000: 0.1 s and 0.15 s). This is now stated in the kill-switch guide and in the known limitations, and a guard test on SQLite (and on Postgres with `PG_DSN`) fails if 10,000 junk entries push any of these calls past 2 seconds or make one grow faster than linearly from 2,500 entries. Keep the control run's store writable only by the processes that operate the kill switch.
- A kill switch stop is now named by its scope, its compensation and a generation (`@stop/set/<scope key>/<generation>`, the generation in the signed record as `gen`) instead of by its content, and the insert is once-only, so two operators who set the same scope at the same instant can no longer leave it in force twice: the loser of the insert reads the control run again, finds the winner's stop in force, and writes nothing. `StopControl.SetStop` returns the stop in force with an `*AlreadyStoppedError` (`errors.Is(err, ErrAlreadyStopped)`) when its scope and compensation are already stopped, and `bide stop` reports "already stopped" and exits 0 as before. A writer with store access and no key cannot block a stop: only a set that decodes, validates and is named by its own content moves the generation (a junk entry under a generation name is stepped over and never counted, a number past 2^31-1 or not canonical is not a generation), and a writer that loses the insert eight times, or finds no generation free, writes the stop under its content-derived name, as every stop was before generations, which cannot be taken (such a stop can duplicate one written at the same moment, the safe direction). An upgrade that meets plain duplicate stops of one scope adopts the scope once. Records written before generations keep their old names and are read as before; the fold is unchanged. Model 16 gains the generation rule (`SetInsertable`, the invariant `OneActivePerScope`, the configs `stop-same-scope` and `stop-same-scope-reach` and the regress `no-generation`). Guide: docs/guides/kill-switch.md.
- The fallback for a run or call id that cannot be shown or pasted safely (a control, bidirectional or invisible character) now says what to do instead of "no command (unsafe id)" or "decide from the application": take the ids from `bide approve -list -json` (the `run` and `call` members) and pass them to `-run` and `-call`, which a test shows records a decision on such a call. In `bide view`'s Needs attention list, a gate with both bare-key and certificate-bound approvers also names which approvers the missing command affects and how each kind decides (`-key`, or `-cert` and `-identity-policy`). The `bide approve -list` help and the guides say the same.

### Fixed

- `scripts/release.sh --assemble-changelog` (the changelog tool's `assemble`) now accepts a pre-release version such as `v0.21.0-rc.1`, read as `scripts/release.sh` reads versions (no leading zeros, a pre-release of non-empty dot-separated identifiers), and refuses a malformed one. The candidate's section sits above the last release, the `[Unreleased]` link moves to its tag, and a later assembly (the next candidate or the final release) finds the candidate's section and link as the release before it. Before, assembling a candidate failed with "version is not like 0.16.0" and its section had to be retitled by hand.
- The CRL refresher's "no valid CRL obtained" error and `Health().LastError` now say why a CRL that was fetched fine was not accepted (a rollback, a conflict between two CRLs of one number, a future-dated, unverifiable or set-aside CRL) instead of listing the URL as "ok"; the per-URL outcome kind is `refused` with the reason in its detail. This also fixes an intermittent failure of the identity reset test, which republished a CRL under the same number with a thisUpdate one second later when it straddled a second boundary and so, correctly, hit the equivocation rule; the test now keeps one base time.
- The status lines of three design notes no longer say nothing is built: `docs/design/approver-identity.md` now says certificate-bound approvers work in gates (v0.19.0) with the CRL refresher (v0.20.0) and lists what is not built yet (RFC 3161 time in gates and SSO issuance; `bide-hsm csr` and `bide identity check` are in progress), `docs/design/visual-editor.md` says phase 1 (`bide edit`) is built, and `docs/design/tooling-and-onboarding.md` says it shipped in v0.14.0.
- The certificate approvers guide and the `bide identity fetch-crl` start-up events told operators to compare the policy digest the command prints with the digest the gate records, but the two can never be equal: the digest covers `effective_from`, which the command's stand-in approver sets to its start time, and values the `-params` file cannot hold (`w`, `min_w`, `routine_grace`, `clock_tolerance`, holds, retention), which the command fills with defaults. The guide and the start-up event now say so, and a new `declared values` start-up event prints the stand-in approver's policy record without `effective_from`, so the timings and pinned CAs can be compared field by field with the policy record the gate journals.
- awss3.LoadDir, and so bide-anchor verify and export -dir, passed on an empty directory and loaded two validly signed heads of one size with different roots as two heads, without an error; an empty directory is now an error wrapping ErrNotFound (nothing to verify never passes), and such a pair in one partition is an error wrapping ErrEquivocation, as the online Reader already reported. The directory walk, the per-partition equivocation check and the empty-directory check now live in audit/anchorobj (Layout.LoadDir, with MaxObjectBytes) so that every object-store anchor gets them, and a directory copy still cannot show that it is complete: compare its newest head with the live bucket.
- The relay's state file write copes with another process holding the file open on Windows: replacing `relay.state` fails with a sharing violation or access denied while a reader (an operator's `tail`, a virus scan) has it open, and the save is now retried on a timer within milliseconds (about 300 ms in all, not logged, no request waits for it). A save that fails for any other reason is no longer forgotten until the next event: the state stays pending and is written again after the flush delay (at least a second, doubling to 30 seconds while it keeps failing, logged once per streak and again when it recovers), or at `Close`. The test that polled the file for the flush no longer fails on the same transient open error.
- Corrects the roadmap and `docs/KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md`, which still said gates do not use the certificate-bound approver checks: they have since v0.19.0 (v0.20.0 added the CRL refresher), and the roadmap now lists what is left (the RFC 3161 verifier is not called by any gate yet, `bide identity check`, `bide-hsm csr`, keys issued through SSO).
- The stop trail's append now retries after losing a race for its position until its caller's deadline (30 seconds when the caller has none), waiting a jittered, growing time between tries, instead of giving up after eight tries. With ten operators writing at once the eight tries ran out (under the race detector, in a test), and a trail write that gives up turns a stop that is in force into exit 5 of `bide stop`; thirty-two concurrent writers now all land. A stop's own trail write keeps trying for 5 seconds (the stop is already in force, and a write that gives up is exit 5, which `bide stop -reaudit` repairs); a lift and the catch-up records before it share one 30-second budget; a caller that cancels gets an error that satisfies `errors.Is(err, context.Canceled)` as well as `ErrTrailConflict`; and `bide stop` prints "in force" the moment the stop is in the control run, before the trail write (`agent.WithStopInForce`).
- The release's `go mod tidy` made `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5` a direct requirement of `cmd/bide` and failed `TestDirectRequires`, because a journalcmd test imported its `stdlib` package directly while `go.mod` listed it as indirect; the test now relies on the driver `store/postgres` registers, and the stale indirect requirements in four example modules are tidied. CI now runs `go mod tidy` in every module and fails if it changes a `go.mod` or `go.sum`, so a direct import of a module marked indirect fails on the pull request and not at release time.
- `bide edit` says so when git did not answer within its 15 second deadline ("git did not answer within 15s and was stopped"), instead of the "signal: killed" the kill leaves, still falls back to the file as it was opened, and no longer waits on a helper process that a git left holding its pipes (the wait is bounded to 2 seconds; a git that answered and exited 0 still counts as an answer). Test only: the stand-in git of the baseline tests is written and run once before the test uses it, because the first run of a newly written executable took 18 seconds on a loaded machine and the release run killed every case at the deadline.

## [0.20.0] - 2026-10-16

### Added

- `bide edit` accessibility pass, the phase gate of the editor design: the outline is one Tab stop moved by the arrow keys, Home and End, the nodes in the drawing are buttons named by their drawn details, the save dialog returns the focus however it closes, and the controls meet the 24-pixel target size and name-contains-label rules. A Chrome test drives the whole path with the keyboard alone (select a node, set its safety, edit its gate, add an assertion, undo, save with the confirmation a weakening needs; the script never uses the pointer and bounds the Tab presses) and another runs axe-core (pinned by the lockfile) over the page in light and dark in five states and fails on any violation. The flow editor guide gains an accessibility section and a policy owner's workflow, `docs/KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` says no manual screen-reader pass has been done and lists what to check by hand, and the README and its four translations list the guide.
- `bide edit`'s assertions panel lists the config's `assert` lines with their verdicts and counterexamples and adds, edits and removes them from the six forms of the grammar as templates (every path to a node passes an approval gate of at least K [of N] [excluding the requester], every path to a node passes another node, every path to a terminal passes a node, no side effect after a node, a node reachable, a node unreachable). The page sends the form's parameters and the server writes the line the check reads (`flowedit.Assertion`, `Assertion.Text`, `ParseAssertion`), quoting a node name with a space and refusing one the grammar cannot hold; only declared nodes can be chosen and a line the config already has is refused. Each change to the form asks the server what the check says of the line and shows its verdict, with the counterexample path of a violation, before anything is added. Add, set and remove are splices (`add_assertion`, `set_assertion` and `remove_assertion` of `flowedit.ApplyOperation`; removing the last line takes the list), so a comment above a removed line goes with it. The panel says assertions are checked in CI and not at run time; `POST /api/apply` answers a refused operation with a 200 and `{"refused": reason}` instead of an error status, so asking what an operation would do does not fill the browser console with failed requests.
- `bide edit` compares the file with a baseline, the committed policy (the file at git `HEAD`, or at the revision `-base <rev>` names; for a file that is not in git, the file as it was when the editor opened it), with the classifier `bide check -diff -check -fail-on-weaken` runs. A text that is weaker than the baseline shows a banner (`APPROVAL GATE WEAKENED` or `SAFETY CHECK WEAKENED`, each item in the classifier's words) that has no close control, stays until the text no longer weakens and is announced as an alert; a text that cannot be read says it cannot be compared. The `flowedit` module gains `flowedit.ApplyOperation`, the editor's operations on a config's text (a node's safety and block, and its approval gate: `need`, approvers, `exclude_principal`, `timeout`) as verified splices so the diff of an edit is the edit and a gate read back through the check is the gate asked for, an `/api/apply` endpoint for it, `flowedit.Compare` and `flowedit.WithBase`. The page does not offer the operations yet.
- `bide edit`'s inspector has forms for the selected node's safety, block and approval gate. A form change is an operation the server applies to the file's text as a splice (`flowedit.ApplyOperation`), verified before it is returned, so the diff of an edit is the edit and comments, blank lines and the style of the lines around it stay; an operation the engine cannot splice safely is refused with the reason. The gate form edits a draft of the decisions needed, the approvers in policy order, separation of duties (`exclude_principal`) and the deadline (`timeout`), and each change to the draft asks the server what `bide check` says of the node with that gate and shows the answer under the form in the check's words (an out-of-range `need`, a repeated approver, an `assert` line the draft would break), so there is no second validator in the page. Apply writes the draft, Remove gate removes the gate, Undo and Redo restore the exact earlier text, a change returns the focus to the control, and the weakened banner follows each change against the baseline. The node's gate as the config declares it is in the view (`Node.Approval`).
- `bide edit` lets you edit the config's source in the page and save it. Typing asks the server for the `bide check` verdict of the new text, so the graph, outline, assertions, problems and the weakened banner follow the text; nothing is written until Save, which opens a dialog with the plain unified diff of the bytes against the file on disk, every comment the diff removes, the number of errors (a file with errors can be saved, and the dialog says it will fail `bide check`), and, for a text weaker than the baseline or one that could not be compared, the items to confirm: the server refuses a save without exactly that confirmation, and one made for an older text does not cover a newer one (HTTP 422, nothing written). The write is atomic (a temporary file in the same directory renamed over the file, keeping its permission bits, leaving the old file and no debris on a failure); the page carries the hash of the file as it read it, and a file that changed on disk since is a conflict (HTTP 409) that shows the file against the author's text and is never overwritten; the path is fixed at startup and checked again at write time (a symbolic link or a directory in its place is refused). The editor never runs git. New in the `flowedit` module: `(*Server).Preview` and `(*Server).Save`, `UnifiedDiff` and `RemovedComments`; the guide and `docs/KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` say what a rename-over save does not keep (hard links, extended attributes, ACLs) and that the check is compare-and-swap, not a lock.
- A CRL refresher for certificate-bound approvers, so a bank's platform team does not write its own CRL fetching loop. `crlrefresh.New(approver, sources, opts)` keeps the approver's `StatusSnapshot` supplied with the newest CRLs of the issuers the policy pins (`Run`, or `RefreshOnce` from cron): it fetches only from the URLs the policy file's `crl` member (or `audit.CRLSources`) names, over HTTP or HTTPS (LDAP is refused), with a size limit, timeouts, no compression and redirects only to the same host; verifies each CRL against its own issuer's certificate (the policy pins intermediates by key, so `intermediate_certs` supplies their certificates) before adding it; refuses a CRL with a lower number than the highest accepted (rollback) and a same-number CRL with another TBSCertList (equivocation); asks every configured URL and keeps the highest valid one; pairs delta CRLs with their base; archives every accepted CRL in a dated, atomically written `crlrefresh.DirArchive` and rebuilds its rollback memory and the snapshot from it at start; and never makes status look fresher, because freshness is read only from each CRL's own dates. Events and alarms go to hooks, `Health()` reports the age and the next expected CRL, and the alarm is `min(max(limit/2, CAPeriod + overlap + tolerance), limit - interval)` with a `Critical` state that is exactly the status rule's own freshness test: a literal 50 percent of validity would alarm every cycle on a healthy weekly root, so the guide now advises a two-period validity for the root CRL with its `ExecStatusAgeFor` set to it. `bide identity fetch-crl` runs the same code for cron (`-once`) or as a daemon, `bide identity reset-high-water` and `ack-reset` are the audited way out of a CA's mistaken huge CRL number, and `bide approve -cert` still fetches nothing. The CRL URLs are not part of the policy digest because they cannot change a verdict.

### Changed

- A node name in double quotes in an `assert` line is now read exactly as written: the assertion grammar has no escapes. Before, `plan` read a quoted name with `strconv.Unquote`, so a name such as `"caf\u00e9"` or `"a\tb"` was unescaped to the character, and a name with a backslash and a space (`my\ node`, `x\n y`) in quotes was read as an undeclared node or as a name with a newline in it; `RenameAssertion` and `CompareAssertions` read it the same way. A quoted name is now read raw by the check, by `RenameAssertion` and `CompareAssertions`, and by the flow editor, which writes the same lines; a bare name was already raw. **Upgrade note:** an assertion that relied on an escape inside the quotes now names a different node; write the character itself (`"café"`). `bide check` warns on every quoted name that contains a backslash sequence the old reading would have changed, so no assertion silently starts naming another node (a warning, not an error, unless `-strict`). A name that contains a double quote still cannot be written in an assertion.

### Fixed

- `TestNotifier_AnAnchorOutageNeverFailsADelivery` failed on Windows because it read the store's `OnError` reports without waiting for the background publisher, and because a failed catch-up from `FlushNotifications` can open the anchor breaker first, in which case the outage is reported to the notifier's error handler and the publisher is skipped. The test now flushes the store and accepts the first report on either channel, a new test pins the breaker ordering, and the `AuditedEventStore.Flush` godoc says that `Agent.FlushNotifications` does not wait for the publisher. No production behavior changed.
- Counts in the `bide edit` page and the `bide view` pages now agree with their nouns: the config verdict reads "1 assertion holds" (not "1 assertion hold"), and the key numbers, the graph label, the audit proof summary, the gate status and the diff error note use the singular for a count of one ("1 node", "1 approval gate", "1 approver", "1 error"). A test pins both forms of each string.

## [0.19.0] - 2026-10-16

### Added

- `bide edit flow.yaml`, the first release of the flow editor (`docs/design/visual-editor.md`), is read-only: a local page, served by the binary on a loopback address, shows a flow config's declared graph, an outline of its nodes and wiring in config order, the selected node's block, safety, crash behavior and approval gate, the assertions with their verdicts and counterexamples, the problems `bide check` finds and the source, all computed by the code `bide check` and `bide view -config` run and labeled as the structural check. The page is hand-written script and the viewer's styles, served from the binary under a Content-Security-Policy that allows only same-origin resources; every request is a POST of JSON from its own origin with a per-run session token (printed in the address's fragment), and the command reads only the file it is given (a `.yaml`, `.yml` or `.json` regular file of at most 1 MiB). It is in the full `bide` binary and not in a `bide_audit_only` build. A Go program can embed the page: `flowedit.New(path).Handler()` is an `http.Handler`, and `flowedit.BuildView` is the page's data for a text. A new Chrome job (`Editor (Chrome)`) drives the page and fails on a console error, a Content-Security-Policy violation or a request off the page's origin. Documented in the new [flow editor guide](docs/guides/flow-editor.md).
- New module `github.com/bide-ai/bide/flowedit`, the edit engine of the planned flow editor (`docs/design/visual-editor.md`): `flowedit.Apply` changes one value, entry or item of a flow config's YAML or JSON text by splicing, so every byte outside the edit is the original byte, comments, key order, flow-style mappings and blank lines survive, and the diff of an edit is the edit. It returns text only after `Verify` re-parses it and checks that its data, key order, untouched lines and comment ownership are the old text with the operation applied; anything it cannot splice safely (an anchor or alias anywhere in the document, merge keys, a repeated key or a key that is not a string, NaN and infinity, a string that is not UTF-8, tags, more than one document or text after the document, mixed line ends, non-UTF-8 text, a document that is not a mapping) is a `*Refusal` and the text is untouched. A file with CRLF line ends or a byte-order mark is edited exactly as the same file with LF ends. All text leaves through one function that a test pins. Measured over 367 configs and 65,951 operations, 99.2 percent of operations applied and touched only the intended lines, against 18.4 percent for a re-emit through `yaml.Node`, with no unverified output in 4.5 million fuzz executions. There is no command yet; `bide edit` follows. It is not published yet: it joins the published modules when `bide edit` requires it.
- Signing certificate-bound decisions from the command line, in the approver identity feature. `bide approve -cert chain.pem -identity-policy policy.json` (and `bide-hsm approve` with a key in a PKCS#11 token or a KMS) signs a decision over the `bide.approval.v4` bytes with the approver's own key and records it with the certificate chain, to a journal or through the approvals intake (`-to`). Before anything is signed it compares the key with the leaf certificate's key (the keys, not a signature), checks the certificate is valid now with at least `-cert-min-left` left, and checks the chain against the policy for `-as`; it reads the chain file before the HSM or KMS is opened, accepts only CERTIFICATE blocks (a private key block is refused unread, at most 8 certificates of 16 KiB), and never falls back to a bare-key decision. It refuses a bare-key decision by an approver the journal says is certificate-bound, and `-cert` by one it says is not; through the intake it checks the gate's identity rule from `GET /pending`, answers a 503 (revocation status not available yet) with a retry instruction and checks the returned receipt carries the chain and verifies under the leaf's key. New `audit.CertDecisionKey`. The browser approve page tells a certificate-bound approver to use `bide approve -to ... -cert`. `bide keygen -csr request.pem` also writes a PKCS#10 certificate signing request for the new Ed25519 or ECDSA P-256 key (proof of possession, no names, never overwrites). `bide approve -list` and the Needs attention list of `bide view` offer the `bide approve -cert` command, with placeholders, to a certificate-bound pending approver (they offered none). The certificate-approvers guide gains a walkthrough from the CA's certificate to an approved call.
- The counting rule for certificate-bound approvers in the planned approver identity feature (build step 1; nothing wires it into a gate yet, so no existing policy changes). `agent.CertDecisionVerifier` is an optional extension of `ApproverVerifier`: `TallyApprovals` checks a decision by the kind of the approver's verifier, never by what the record carries, and gives the verifier the whole journal and the record's position in it. `audit.CertApprover` implements it: a decision counts when it carries a chain and is signed over the `bide.approval.v4` bytes, a signed head of the run covers it (the smallest covering head fixes the time it became provable, and heads that contradict each other refuse it), the chain is accepted for the approver at that time, and revocation status allows it. The seat is the verified identity (`ident:` and the canonical identifier), one leaf key fills one seat, and separation of duties excludes a requester by identifier. A `DecisionCheck` of such a decision carries the finding: identity, tier, basis, status time and when it became provable. New `agent.ReasonNoCoveringHead` and `ReasonHeadTimes`. A gate with a certificate-bound approver needs the identity map to name every approver of the policy, bare-key ones included, and until the gate change is complete a gate refuses a certificate-bound approver at configuration (`ErrConfig`), so the counting rule is not reachable from a gate yet. Separation of duties also excludes a requester mapped to the decision's identifier through the roster (`CertApprover.RequesterIdentity`), with a warning on the finding for a requester it cannot map.
- Conformance captures of two-tier approver PKIs for the planned certificate-bound approver identity status rule, with true delta CRLs: EJBCA Community 9.6.3 (an RSA-3072 root and an ECDSA P-256 issuing CA) and Microsoft AD CS on Windows Server 2025 (an RSA-3072 standalone root and standalone subordinate on two machines), captured by the manually dispatched workflow `crl-fixtures.yml`, checked in under `audit/testdata/ejbca` and `audit/testdata/adcs`, and exercised offline by tests that run `VerifyChain` and the status rule on the real certificates and CRLs, including the base and delta pairing, cumulative deltas, AD CS's ten minute back-dating of `thisUpdate` and `notBefore`, key compromise, supersession and hold release. New `IdentityPolicy.NotBeforeBackdate` declares a CA's notBefore back-dating so a 24 hour certificate that spans 24 hours and ten minutes is not refused by `MaxLeafLifetime`, and `StatusParams.MaxStatusAgeFor` gives an offline root's weekly CRL its own age limit; a CRL with an entry dated more than 30 minutes after its own `nextUpdate` is now refused, and a declared `ThisUpdateSkew` or `MaxStatusAgeFor` that is negative or over its cap makes the status unknown.
- Delta CRL merging for the planned certificate-bound approver identity status rule (build step 1, not yet used by the approval gate, so no behaviour changes). `StatusSnapshot.AddCRL` accepts a delta CRL with a critical DER INTEGER delta indicator; evaluating a chain pairs each delta with the highest qualifying base (RFC 5280 sections 5.2.4 and 6.3.3), reads the merged view as of the delta with the earlier of the two nextUpdate values, treats numbers as one sequence across bases and deltas, reads the listings of every delta that verifies (an unpaired delta gives no evidence of absence), and treats holds as unaccepted by default (a certificate that was ever on hold is refused unless `StatusParams.AllowHolds`, which then refuses a decision only inside the hold). OpenSSL-written certificates and CRLs, including overlapping issuance and refusals on real output, are checked in as conformance fixtures (`audit/testdata/openssl`, `gen.sh` regenerates them). `StatusParams.ThisUpdateSkew` (and `ThisUpdateSkewFor`, per issuing CA) declares how much earlier than its real issue a CA stamps `thisUpdate` (Microsoft AD CS back-dates it by about ten minutes): the release of a hold by absence ends at the next CRL's `thisUpdate` plus the skew, and a CRL's age is measured from the same moment; zero is never assumed.
- Approver identity in the run viewer and the approval commands (build step E3 of the planned approver identity feature; the gate that writes the identity records is a separate step, so no existing gate changes). `bide view` shows, for a gate with a certificate-bound approver, the verified identifier, the certificate's display name, the tier (final as recorded with an independent or log-signer time, or PROVISIONAL), the basis, the status time S, when the decision became provable and a dispute (a keyCompromise voiding back to notBefore) on the run page, in the Needs attention list and in the JSON view; a gate paused for status appears as "paused: identity status unknown", and a call held before execution is listed. Nothing is drawn green or called verified without an audit result the viewer is given: the journal alone gives final as recorded, in a neutral style, and provisional, recorded-but-not-counted, held, incomplete (an identity tally with no record naming it) or policy-mismatched identity are each visibly non-final, so a finished run is never VERIFIED on the gate's word (COMPLETED, or IDENTITY NOT FINAL). A dispute is shown only from an audit result. `bide approve -list` prints the same and never shows an identity-bound gate as verified or settled. `bide approve -verify-receipt` (new `-identity-policy` and `-log-key`) and `bide audit verify-receipts` (new `-identity-policy`) now check a certificate-bound receipt against a certificate policy file at the time the decision became provable, and say that revocation status was not checked. New `audit.ParseIdentityPolicy` (the policy file) and `audit.DecisionReceipt.VerifyIdentity`.
- The check of certificate-bound approvers' decisions immediately before a gated call runs, and the operator guide, in the approver identity feature (this change turns the feature on for gates: a policy whose resolver returns an `audit.CertApprover` is now accepted). One function, `preExecute`, re-verifies every counted certificate-bound decision of the gate's outcome with the execution ages and sits on every path to the tool: the base tool handler before the call is begun (so the first execution, a resume, crash recovery, `RecoverLoop` and a middleware retry all pass it) and a plan flow node before its body runs. A decision that is revoked (`E <= A(d)`) or no longer verifies for good writes an insert-once exec hold (`ApprovalExecHoldStep`, with the certificate serial, `E`, reason code and CRL hash) and the next generation is counted, so replacement decisions run the call; status that is missing or stale, or a decision no head covers right now, only pauses (`agent.PendingIdentityStatus`, no record, no approval notification). `audit.CertApprover.VerifyCertExec` applies two tiers by position in the accepted chain: the leaf's issuing CA is judged by `ExecStatusAge`, every CA above it by its own `ExecStatusAgeFor`, which `ValidateConfig` requires with `CAPeriodFor`, `MaxStatusAgeFor` and the new `CAOverlapFor` (`CAPeriodFor + CAOverlapFor <= ExecStatusAgeFor <= MaxStatusAgeFor`); the new values, `MaxStatusAgeFor` and `NotBeforeBackdate` are in the recorded policy and its digest. New `CertApprover.ExecNow` (the execution check's clock), `agent.CertExecVerifier`, `agent.CheckExec`, `agent.CertRevocation` and `audit.ExecLatencyBound`. The guide `docs/guides/certificate-approvers.md` covers declaring the CA's facts from a capture of your own CA, publishing the CRL right after revoking, provisional and final counts, and both revocation latency bounds.
- The gate-side records of the planned approver identity feature (build step 1; certificate-bound approvers stay refused at gate configuration, `ErrConfig`, until the last change of the feature, so nothing reaches a bare-key policy). A gate with a certificate-bound approver anchors first: a decision that verifies but has no covering signed head makes the gate ask the store to publish one (`Reanchorer`, at most once per 10 s per run), and "no covering head" is reported only for a decision whose chain, identifier, scheme and signature already verify, so a junk decision never makes the gate sign. It writes phase notes (`awaiting-anchor`, `awaiting-status`) and journals, before the tally, the CRLs the count rests on by hash (`approval-status:<sha256>`, a CRL above 256 KiB goes to `CertApprover.StatusArchive`) and an `approval-identity:<id>:<generation>` record: the policy in force with every verdict-affecting parameter, the roster digest, and for each counted decision its chain hashes, anchor, covering head, time source, tier, basis and status hashes, bound to the tally by a digest of its stored bytes. `StatusParams.Validate` and `CertApprover.ValidateConfig` (including `CAPeriod <= ExecStatusAge/2` for the leaf's issuer and a bounded status snapshot or an archive) run when the gate is configured.
- A certificate-bound approver's decision can be taken over HTTP and checked before it is written. `agent.WithDecisionCheck` and `SubmitDecision` now verify a decision that carries a certificate chain through the approver's verifier (`agent.CertIntakeVerifier`, implemented by `audit.CertApprover.VerifyCertIntake`: the chain accepted for the approver at the time of the write, the `bide.approval.v4` signature under the leaf's key, status usable now) instead of refusing it, and write nothing on any failure; status that is missing or stale is the new `agent.ErrStatusUnavailable`. The approvals intake takes the chain in the request (`cert`, a base64 DER array, leaf first, at most 8 certificates of 16 KiB, bounded before any parsing), answers 503 `status_unavailable` with `Retry-After` and writes nothing when status cannot be judged, 422 for an invalid or revoked chain or a downgrade (a certificate-bound approver without a chain, a bare-key approver with one), answers a replay of a held decision from the record even during an outage, and anchors after the write. `counted: true` on a certificate-bound decision is not final: each decision carries `tier`, `provisional` and `basis`, the response carries `anchored`, `GET /pending` lists the identifier each certificate-bound approver's certificate must carry (`gate.identity`), and receipts carry the chain. `agent.Journal.Reanchor` asks the store to publish a head.
- Model 15 (`spec/tla/identity`) gains a CA tier: the leaf's issuing CA is certified by a root that publishes its own, slower CRL series, may withhold a CRL and may revoke the CA with a past effective time, and the count and the pre-execution check read each tier at its own status age (`MaxAge` and `ExecAge` for the leaf's issuer, `MaxAgeCA` and `ExecAgeCA` for the CA, as the two-tier `ExecStatusAgeFor` design has it). `NoExecAfterKnownRevocation` is checked per tier (`NoExecAfterLeafTierRevocation`, `NoExecAfterCATierRevocation`) by `id-ca-tier`, and three counterexample configurations fail with the property they guard: a check that reads only the leaf tier, a count that ignores the root, and the leaf aged by the CA tier's longer age. A final verdict also reads the CA tier (a root CRL a window past the decision and none that lists the CA; `id-final-ca`, `final-no-ca-tier`), and the tally recheck covers the root archive. The nightly identity state counts in the model 15 README are re-measured.
- A Go refinement test for formal model 15 (approver identity), `audit.TestModel15Refinement`: each counterexample configuration of the model (`spec/tla/identity/regress` and `limits`) has a scenario that runs the same situation through the real counting rule (`agent.TallyApprovals` with `audit.CertApprover`, a stdlib CA, signed heads and CRLs) and compares the counted set with what the model's rule gives, so a rule switched off in the model fails with the property it guards and the code keeps the rule on. Each scenario also runs a positive control (the same setup with the rule satisfied) that must count or be final, and the six pre-execution configurations run through the gate's one pre-execution path and the verifier's check, which must agree. A drift guard fails when a model configuration has no scenario (the alias limit is documented, not checked) or a scenario names none. `CertApprover.VerifyCertDecision` is marked as model 15's `Count`.
- Formal model 15 (`spec/tla/identity`) checks the approver-identity design's counting rule before its Go code lands: certificates with a holder, an identifier, a validity interval and a revocation effective time, a CRL series with numbers, signed journal heads, and decisions that carry a certificate, under a CA that may mis-issue, a CRL that may be withheld or rolled back, a head stamped out of order, and a decision with an earlier `decided_at`.
